# An autonomous Lipschitz fast dynamo on the three-torus

**arXiv ID**: 2608.02586v1
**Authors**: Lukas Niebel
**Published**: 2026-08-03
**Categories**: math.AP, math-ph, math.SP
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.02586v1

## Abstract

We construct a single real-valued, divergence-free, time-independent velocity field $u\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^3;\mathbb{R}^3) $ that is a fast dynamo for the kinematic induction equation on the flat three-torus. For every sufficiently small positive magnetic diffusivity, the corresponding induction operator has an eigenvalue whose real part is bounded below by a positive constant independent of the diffusivity. For each such diffusivity, there is a non-zero real-valued, divergence-free solution of the induction equation whose $\mathrm{L}^2 $-norm obeys an exact exponential growth law with a uniformly positive rate. For the same velocity field, the particle flow and its inverse have Lipschitz constants growing at most linearly in time, every time map has zero topological entropy, and the ideal induction group has zero exponential growth rate in operator norm. The velocity is differentiable everywhere and smooth away from a single circle, but is not $\mathrm{C}^1 $.

An autonomous Lipschitz fast dynamo on the three-torus

## An autonomous Lipschitz fast dynamo on the three-torusLukas NiebelETH Zürich, Department of Mathematics, Rämistrasse 101, 8092 Zürich, Switzerland.lukas.niebel@math.ethz.ch(Date:3rd August 2026)

## Abstract.

We construct a single real-valued, divergence-free, time-independent velocity fieldu∈W1,∞​(𝕋3;ℝ3)u\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})that is a fast dynamo for the kinematic induction equation on the flat three-torus. For every sufficiently small positive magnetic diffusivity, the corresponding induction operator has an eigenvalue whose real part is bounded below by a positive constant independent of the diffusivity. For each such diffusivity, there is a non-zero real-valued, divergence-free solution of the induction equation whoseL2\mathrm{L}^{2}-norm obeys an exact exponential growth law with a uniformly positive rate. For the same velocity field, the particle flow and its inverse have Lipschitz constants growing at most linearly in time, every time map has zero topological entropy, and the ideal induction group has zero exponential growth rate in operator norm. The velocity is differentiable everywhere and smooth away from a single circle, but is notC1\mathrm{C}^{1}.

## Key words and phrases:fast dynamo, kinematic induction equation, Ponomarenko dynamo, vanishing magnetic diffusivity, spectral instability, topological entropy

## 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification:76W05 (Primary) 35P05, 47A10, 35B25, 37B40 (Secondary)

## 1.Introduction and main result

## 1.1.The induction equation and the fast-dynamo question

We work on the flat unit torus𝕋3=ℝ3/ℤ3\mathbb{T}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{3}/\mathbb{Z}^{3}, equipped with normalised Lebesgue measure, and writex=(y,x3)x=(y,x_{3})withy=(x1,x2)∈𝕋2y=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}. We call thex3x_{3}-direction axial and writee3=(0,0,1)e_{3}=(0,0,1). We write∥⋅∥2\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}for theL2\mathrm{L}^{2}-norm. Given a divergence-free
velocity fielduuand a magnetic diffusivityε>0\varepsilon>0, the magnetic
fieldBBsolves{∂tB=curl⁡(u×B)+ε​Δ​B,div⁡B=0,B|t=0=Bin.\begin{cases}\partial_{t}B=\operatorname{curl}(u\times B)+\varepsilon\Delta B,\\
\operatorname{div}B=0,\\
B|_{t=0}=B_{\rm in}.\end{cases}(1.1)

This is thekinematicdynamo problem: the velocity is prescribed,
and the magnetic field does not feed back into its evolution. Thus all
growth below is produced by the linear equation (1.1) for
one fixeduu. The vector identitycurl⁡(u×B)=u​div⁡B−B​div⁡u+(B⋅∇)​u−(u⋅∇)​B\operatorname{curl}(u\times B)=u\,\operatorname{div}B-B\,\operatorname{div}u+(B\cdot\nabla)u-(u\cdot\nabla)B

shows that, ifuuandBBare divergence-free, the first equation is
equivalently∂tB+(u⋅∇)​B−(B⋅∇)​u=ε​Δ​B.\partial_{t}B+(u\cdot\nabla)B-(B\cdot\nabla)u=\varepsilon\Delta B.

The transport term moves the magnetic field, the stretching term(B⋅∇)​u(B\cdot\nabla)umay amplify it, and diffusion damps the small scales
created by stretching. Formally, their competition is expressed by the
energy identity12​dd​t​‖B​(t)‖22=∫𝕋3B⋅(B⋅∇)​u​dx−ε​‖∇B‖22.\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\left\|B(t)\right\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{3}}B\cdot(B\cdot\nabla)u\,\mathrm{d}x-\varepsilon\left\|\nabla B\right\|_{2}^{2}.(1.2)

For a time-independent velocity field, letLε,u​B:=curl⁡(u×B)+ε​Δ​BL_{\varepsilon,u}B:=\operatorname{curl}(u\times B)+\varepsilon\Delta B(1.3)

on the complex Hilbert spaceLσ2​(𝕋3):={B∈L2​(𝕋3;ℂ3):div⁡B=0},\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3}):=\{B\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}):\operatorname{div}B=0\},

with domainH2​(𝕋3;ℂ3)∩Lσ2​(𝕋3)\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3}).
All complex inner products are linear in their first argument.
We omit the domain from Sobolev spaces and norms whenever it is clear from
context.

The coefficientε\varepsilonis the magnetic diffusion coefficient. For fixed
magnetic permeability, it is inversely proportional to electrical
conductivity, soε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0is the high-conductivity limit. We useidealfor the equation withε=0\varepsilon=0andresistivefor the
equation withε>0\varepsilon>0.

The distinction between slow and fast dynamos, introduced by Vainshtein
and Zel’dovich[48], concerns whether the sustained exponential
growth rate survives this high-conductivity limit. A fast dynamo has a
uniformly positive lower rate and therefore grows on a time scale that
stays bounded asε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0. A slow dynamo has a rate tending to zero;
its diverging amplification time need not coincide with the pure diffusive,
or resistive, time scale. For a fixed autonomous velocity field, define the
maximal sustained growth rate byγ​(u,ε):=limt→∞1t​log⁡‖et​Lε,u‖Lσ2→Lσ2.\gamma(u,\varepsilon):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\left\|\mathrm{e}^{tL_{\varepsilon,u}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}\to\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}}.(1.4)

The limit exists because the logarithm of the semigroup norm is
subadditive and everyC0\mathrm{C}_{0}-semigroup is locally bounded in time[21, Proposition IV.2.2].
It records long-time exponential growth and ignores bounded transient
amplification.

We calluua dynamo at diffusivityε\varepsilonwhenγ​(u,ε)>0\gamma(u,\varepsilon)>0. It is afast dynamoiflim infε↓0γ​(u,ε)>0.\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\gamma(u,\varepsilon)>0.

Ifγ​(u,ε)>0\gamma(u,\varepsilon)>0for every sufficiently smallε\varepsilon, butγ​(u,ε)→0\gamma(u,\varepsilon)\to 0, we call it aslow dynamo.

The following properties of the semigroup and its spectral growth are proved in AppendixA.

## Lemma 1.1(Semigroup generation and spectral growth).

Letu∈W1,∞​(𝕋3;ℝ3)u\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})be divergence-free and letε>0\varepsilon>0. The operatorLε,uL_{\varepsilon,u}, with domainH2∩Lσ2\mathrm{H}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}, generates an analyticC0\mathrm{C}_{0}-semigroup onLσ2\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}.

More precisely, the operatorL~ε,u​B=curl⁡(u×B)+ε​Δ​B,D​(L~ε,u)=H2​(𝕋3;ℂ3),\widetilde{L}_{\varepsilon,u}B=\operatorname{curl}(u\times B)+\varepsilon\Delta B,\qquad D(\widetilde{L}_{\varepsilon,u})=\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}),

generates an analytic semigroup onL2​(𝕋3;ℂ3)\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}). This
semigroup leavesLσ2\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}invariant, and its restriction iset​Lε,u\mathrm{e}^{tL_{\varepsilon,u}}. The resolvent ofLε,uL_{\varepsilon,u}is compact,et​Lε,u\mathrm{e}^{tL_{\varepsilon,u}}is compact for everyt>0t>0, andspec⁡(et​Lε,u)∖{0}={et​λ:λ∈spec⁡(Lε,u)},t>0.\operatorname{spec}(\mathrm{e}^{tL_{\varepsilon,u}})\setminus\{0\}=\left\{\mathrm{e}^{t\lambda}:\lambda\in\operatorname{spec}(L_{\varepsilon,u})\right\},\qquad t>0.(1.5)

Consequently,γ​(u,ε)=sup{Re⁡λ:λ∈spec⁡(Lε,u)},\gamma(u,\varepsilon)=\sup\{\operatorname{Re}\lambda:\lambda\in\operatorname{spec}(L_{\varepsilon,u})\},(1.6)

with the convention that the supremum of the empty set is−∞-\infty. Whenever the spectrum is nonempty, this supremum is a
maximum.

Thus the fast-dynamo question for an autonomous velocity field is an eigenvalue
problem. On the flat three-torus it is commonly formulated as follows;
see Arnold’s Problem 1994–28[3]and[4, Chapter V].

## Conjecture 1.2(Smooth autonomous fast dynamo).

There exists a single real-valued, time-independent velocity fieldu∈C∞​(𝕋3;ℝ3),div⁡u=0,u\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad\operatorname{div}u=0,

such thatlim infε↓0γ​(u,ε)>0.\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\gamma(u,\varepsilon)>0.

Equivalently, one asks for one smooth velocity fielduuand constantsε0,γ0>0\varepsilon_{0},\gamma_{0}>0, all chosen before the diffusivity, such thatLε,uL_{\varepsilon,u}has an eigenvalue in{Re⁡z≥γ0}\{\operatorname{Re}z\geq\gamma_{0}\}for every0<ε≤ε00<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}. Here and below, amagnetic eigenmodeis a
non-zero divergence-free eigenfunctionVVof the relevant induction
operator; if its eigenvalue isλ\lambda, it generates the complex solutiont↦et​λ​Vt\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{t\lambda}V. The eigenvalue and a corresponding magnetic
eigenmode may depend onε\varepsilon. Considering every sufficiently small
diffusivity is essential: growth only along a sequenceεj↓0\varepsilon_{j}\downarrow 0would be a weaker conclusion.

## 1.2.Main theorem

Our result proves this eigenvalue statement with a Lipschitz velocity field. It
also identifies, for each diffusivity, a real magnetic field whose norm
obeys an exact exponential law at every time.

## Theorem 1.3(Autonomous Lipschitz fast dynamo).

There exist a real-valued autonomous velocity fieldu∈W1,∞​(𝕋3;ℝ3),div⁡u=0,u\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad\operatorname{div}u=0,

and constantsε0>0,γ0>0,\varepsilon_{0}>0,\qquad\gamma_{0}>0,

such thatγ​(u,ε)≥γ0for every​0<ε≤ε0,and hencelim infε↓0γ​(u,ε)≥γ0.\gamma(u,\varepsilon)\geq\gamma_{0}\quad\text{for every }0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\gamma(u,\varepsilon)\geq\gamma_{0}.(1.7)

More precisely, for every0<ε≤ε00<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}, there areKε∈ℤ∖{0},λε∈ℂ,0≠bε∈H2​(𝕋2;ℂ3),K_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},\qquad\lambda_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{C},\qquad 0\neq b^{\varepsilon}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}),

such that the complex magnetic eigenmodeVε​(y,x3):=e2​π​i​Kε​x3​bε​(y)∈H2​(𝕋3;ℂ3)∩Lσ2​(𝕋3),Lε,u​Vε=λε​Vε,Re⁡λε≥γ0.V^{\varepsilon}(y,x_{3}):=\mathrm{e}^{2\pi iK_{\varepsilon}x_{3}}b^{\varepsilon}(y)\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3}),\quad L_{\varepsilon,u}V^{\varepsilon}=\lambda_{\varepsilon}V^{\varepsilon},\quad\operatorname{Re}\lambda_{\varepsilon}\geq\gamma_{0}.(1.8)

Moreover, the real-valued datumBinε:=Re⁡Vε∈H2​(𝕋3;ℝ3)∩Lσ2​(𝕋3)B_{\rm in}^{\varepsilon}:=\operatorname{Re}V^{\varepsilon}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3})

is non-zero, and its solution satisfies‖Bε​(t)‖L2​(𝕋3)=et​Re⁡λε​‖Binε‖L2​(𝕋3)for every​t≥0.\left\|B^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}=\mathrm{e}^{t\operatorname{Re}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}\left\|B_{\rm in}^{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})}\qquad\text{for every }t\geq 0.

Before stating the additional dynamical properties of the example, we fix
some terminology. Theparticle flowΦt\Phi_{t}solvesdd​t​Φt​(x)=u​(Φt​(x)),Φ0​(x)=x.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\Phi_{t}(x)=u(\Phi_{t}(x)),\qquad\Phi_{0}(x)=x.

For a Lipschitz mapFF,Lip⁡(F)\operatorname{Lip}(F)denotes its optimal
Lipschitz constant.
The notationhtop​(F)h_{\mathrm{top}}(F)denotes the topological entropy of a
continuous self-mapFFof a compact metric space. It measures the
exponential growth rate of the largest family of orbit segments that can be
distinguished at increasingly fine spatial resolution[9,50]; a definition is given in (3.15).

For the same velocity fielduu,limt→∞1t​log⁡‖𝒯0​(t)‖Lσ2→Lσ2=0<γ0≤lim infε↓0γ​(u,ε).\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\left\|\mathcal{T}_{0}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}\to\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}}=0<\gamma_{0}\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\gamma(u,\varepsilon).

## Proof.

The ideal equality follows from Proposition1.4, and the resistive
lower bound is (1.7).
∎

The magnetic eigenmodeVεV^{\varepsilon}is complex. IfIm⁡λε≠0\operatorname{Im}\lambda_{\varepsilon}\neq 0, its real part is not itself a magnetic eigenmode,
since it is not an eigenfunction ofLε,uL_{\varepsilon,u}. Rather,Re⁡Vε\operatorname{Re}V^{\varepsilon}andIm⁡Vε\operatorname{Im}V^{\varepsilon}span a real
invariant plane on which the evolution is multiplication byet​Re⁡λε\mathrm{e}^{t\operatorname{Re}\lambda_{\varepsilon}}followed by rotation through the anglet​Im⁡λεt\operatorname{Im}\lambda_{\varepsilon}. The non-zero axial Fourier index makes these two
real fields orthogonal with the sameL2\mathrm{L}^{2}-norm. Consequently the
rotation preserves their norm and gives the exact identity in the theorem.
The calculation is given in Section7.

## Remark 1.6(Scope of the spectral conclusion).

The order of the quantifiers in Theorem1.3is∃u∃ε0,γ0>0∀0<ε≤ε0∃Kε,λε,bε∀t≥0.\exists\,u\quad\exists\,\varepsilon_{0},\gamma_{0}>0\quad\forall\,0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\quad\exists\,K_{\varepsilon},\lambda_{\varepsilon},b^{\varepsilon}\quad\forall\,t\geq 0.

HereVεV^{\varepsilon}is the magnetic eigenmode defined fromKεK_{\varepsilon}andbεb^{\varepsilon}in
(1.8). Hence the velocity and the positive
lower bound are independent ofε\varepsilon, whereas the magnetic eigenmode
and the real initial datum may depend onε\varepsilon. The theorem selects some
spectral point and magnetic datum for each diffusivity. It does not assert
one common datum for all
diffusivities, simplicity or continuity ofλε\lambda_{\varepsilon}, or that the
selected eigenvalue attains the spectral bound. SinceKε≠0K_{\varepsilon}\neq 0, every magnetic field constructed here has zero spatial
mean.
The velocity is differentiable everywhere and smooth away from one axial
circle. Its derivative vanishes on that circle but is discontinuous
there, so the velocity is notC1\mathrm{C}^{1}; the direct proof is given in
Subsection3.5. Thus the present theorem reaches the
quantifiers of Conjecture1.2, but not its smoothness
hypothesis.

## Remark 1.7(Normalisation).

For a normalised velocity, putM=max⁡{1,‖u‖W1,∞}M=\max\{1,\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}}\}andu~=u/M\widetilde{u}=u/M. ThenLε,u~=M−1​LM​ε,u,γ​(u~,ε)=M−1​γ​(u,M​ε).L_{\varepsilon,\widetilde{u}}=M^{-1}L_{M\varepsilon,u},\qquad\gamma(\widetilde{u},\varepsilon)=M^{-1}\gamma(u,M\varepsilon).

Hence Theorem1.3remains valid foru~\widetilde{u}, with
diffusivity thresholdε0/M\varepsilon_{0}/Mand growth lower boundγ0/M\gamma_{0}/M. In particular, the velocity may be required to satisfy‖u~‖W1,∞≤1\left\|\widetilde{u}\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}}\leq 1without changing any
qualitative conclusion. Moreover, the particle flow and ideal induction
group foru~\widetilde{u}are the corresponding objects foruuevaluated at timet/Mt/M. Thus Proposition1.4also remains
valid.

The assertions in Proposition1.4are proved from the explicit
cell flow in Proposition3.2and the Cauchy formula in
Corollary3.3, which represents the ideal magnetic field as the
initial field transported and deformed by the particle flow.

The growing magnetic eigenmodeVεV^{\varepsilon}moves to larger axial Fourier
indices and becomes increasingly oscillatory inx3x_{3}asε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0. Here and below,a≍ba\asymp bmeans that their ratio is bounded above and below by positive
constants independent of the parameter under consideration. More precisely,|Kε|≍ε−1/2\left\lvert K_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert\asymp\varepsilon^{-1/2}, and the corresponding
cell has transverse size comparable toε\sqrt{\varepsilon}. Thus the
argument uses successively smaller invariant cells, and the growing initial
datum is allowed to depend onε\varepsilon. The theorem concerns long-time
operator growth after choosing spectral data separately for each
diffusivity. For each fixed initial field, by contrast, the resistive solution
converges strongly inL2\mathrm{L}^{2}, uniformly on bounded time intervals, to the ideal
solution asε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0; this follows from the Trotter–Kato theorem[21, Theorem III.4.8], since the energy estimate gives uniform
semigroup bounds and periodic mollification, together with the Friedrichs
commutator lemma[20, Lemma II.1], gives the required smooth-core convergence.

This does not contradict the classical results that rule out fast dynamo
action for sufficiently smooth flows without exponential particle
stretching. Vishik and Friedlander–Vishik prove such results under
smoothness assumptions[49,22]. Klapper and
Young bound the fast-dynamo rate of aCk+1\mathrm{C}^{k+1}map or flow by its
topological entropy, together with a finite-smoothness correction; forC∞\mathrm{C}^{\infty}flows, zero topological entropy therefore rules out a fast
dynamo[32]. Our velocity is notC1\mathrm{C}^{1}on the
accumulation circle, so these theorems do not apply. The example shows that
their smoothness assumption cannot simply be weakened toW1,∞\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}.

## 1.3.Construction and proof strategy

The main difficulty is the order of the quantifiers. We must construct one
velocity field beforeε\varepsilonis known, while diffusion with coefficientε\varepsilonacts over distances of orderε\sqrt{\varepsilon}on anO​(1)O(1)time
interval. We therefore place copies of one locally supported unstable
profile at infinitely many scales. For every sufficiently smallε\varepsilon,
one copy has the matching scale. The rest of the proof isolates that copy
from all the others without weakening its growth.

We first outline the argument without the technical localisation estimates.

1. Construct one local unstable velocity profile.By alocal unstable velocity profilewe mean a compactly supported
velocity for which
the reduced induction operator has an eigenvalue with positive real part.
We begin on the infinite cylinderℝ2×𝕋\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}. We construct a smoothx3x_{3}-independent screw-type velocity field: its transverse component
rotates around thex3x_{3}-axis, while its third component transports along
the cylinder. We refer to this combination of rotation and axial
translation ashelical. The velocity field is supported in a bounded
transverse disc, and one non-zerox3x_{3}-Fourier mode grows exponentially.

The first calculation is made for a velocity with a jump at the edge of a
cylinder, because separation of variables then reduces the problem to
modified Bessel functions. We then smooth the jump. Rather than
try to follow one eigenvalue through this smoothing, we draw a small circle
around the unstable eigenvalue and keep track of the full generalised
eigenspace inside it. Equivalently, we follow the range of the Riesz
projection associated with that circle. The circle remains in the right
half-plane, so every eigenvalue
it encloses has a positive real part. The same circle continues to work
when the rescaled diffusivity is varied slightly. These conclusions are proved
in Section2.

2. Place copies of the profile at every small scale.Letℓn↓0\ell_{n}\downarrow 0be a geometric sequence. In a disc centred atpnp_{n}, placeunloc​(y)=ℓn​v​(y−pnℓn),u_{n}^{\rm loc}(y)=\ell_{n}v\left(\frac{y-p_{n}}{\ell_{n}}\right),

wherevvis the local velocity field constructed in
Section2. The prefactorℓn\ell_{n}cancels the factorℓn−1\ell_{n}^{-1}produced by a transverse derivative. Thus transport and
stretching remain of order one, while after the change of variablesy=pn+ℓn​Yy=p_{n}+\ell_{n}Ythe diffusivity becomesδn=εℓn2.\delta_{n}=\frac{\varepsilon}{\ell_{n}^{2}}.

We choose the scales so that the intervals of physical diffusivities for
whichδn\delta_{n}lies in the unstable range overlap. Hence every sufficiently
smallε\varepsilonactivates at least one cell.

There is a second matching condition. If the magnetic field has axial
Fourier indexKnK_{n}, then transverse rescaling changes its axial wave
number from2​π​Kn2\pi K_{n}to2​π​Kn​ℓn2\pi K_{n}\ell_{n}. We impose2​π​Kn​ℓn=N=2​π​k∗.2\pi K_{n}\ell_{n}=N=2\pi k_{*}.

Herek∗k_{*}is the fixed local Fourier index andN=2​π​k∗N=2\pi k_{*}is the
corresponding axial wavenumber.
Both the rescaled diffusivity and the rescaled axial wavenumber then
agree with those of the local model. No factor depending onℓn\ell_{n}multiplies time or the spectral parameter, so the positive growth rate does
not shrink with the cell.

3. Provide a buffer around each cell.The support of thenn-th local velocity has sizeℓn\ell_{n}, but we place
it in a larger disc of radiusRn=ℓnR_{n}=\sqrt{\ell_{n}}. The region between
these two scales is a buffer, empty of the local flow. The radiiRnR_{n}are summable, so all discs fit in the torus and accumulate at one
point of𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}. A cell adapted toε\varepsilonsatisfiesε≍ℓn2\varepsilon\asymp\ell_{n}^{2}, and henceRnε⟶∞(ε↓0).\frac{R_{n}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\longrightarrow\infty\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow 0).

On the fixedO​(1)O(1)spectral contour used below, solutions of the
constant-coefficient resolvent equation decay on the transverse length
scaleO​(ε)O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}). A solution generated near the local-flow core is therefore
exponentially small when it reaches the outer edge of the buffer, and a
solution generated outside is exponentially small when it reaches the
local-flow core.

The rescaled velocities have amplitude of orderℓn\ell_{n}and gradients
of order one. Their disjoint sum is consequently Lipschitz and continuous
at the accumulation point. The gradients do not converge there,
which is why the resulting velocity is notC1\mathrm{C}^{1}.

4. Separate the spectra of the other cells.For the Fourier index associated with thenn-th cell, the remaining cells
could in principle have spectrum near the same unstable eigenvalue. We
separate them by adding a very small velocity in the axial direction to
each disc. We call this theadded axial velocity. On a
magnetic Fourier modeB​(y,x3)=e2​π​i​K​x3​b​(y)B(y,x_{3})=\mathrm{e}^{2\pi iKx_{3}}b(y), the added transport
term acts as−(a​e3⋅∇)​B=−2​π​i​K​a​e2​π​i​K​x3​b​(y).-(ae_{3}\cdot\nabla)B=-2\pi iKa\,\mathrm{e}^{2\pi iKx_{3}}b(y).

Thus, on the fixed-KKFourier subspace, adding the constant velocity fielda​e3ae_{3}changes the reduced operator by−2​π​i​K​a​Id-2\pi iKa\,\mathrm{Id}.
This is a purely imaginary scalar shift: it changes temporal phase but not
the real part of any spectral value. We choose the constants so that thenn-th cell receives one fixed shift and the other cells treated separately
are shifted away from the spectral contour. Each added axial velocity is
constant near its local-flow core and is smoothly reduced to zero near the outer edge
of the large buffer. Because this change takes place over the buffer scale,
its contribution to∇u\nabla uis small.

5. Compare the full problem with the isolated cells.We now solve the resolvent equation separately in thenn-th cell, in the
finitely many other cells (all larger cells and a fixed
number of smaller ones), and in a background operator containing the
remaining smaller cells. Cut-off functions join these solutions into an
approximate global solution. Three estimates enter the comparison. The
union of the very small cells has small area, so its stretching contribution
is small in an energy estimate. The added axial velocities give uniform
resolvent bounds for the other cells treated separately. Finally, the
constant-coefficient
buffers make the cut-off errors exponentially small.
After the geometric and spectral parameters have been fixed, choosing the
first scale sufficiently small makes the total error less than one. A
Neumann series then turns the approximate inverse into the true resolvent
of the torus operator.

Finally we integrate the approximate inverse around the translated
spectral contour. The background term and the other local terms integrate
to zero because they have no spectrum inside it, while the term withm=nm=nis non-zero. The true resolvent is close enough to this approximate
inverse that the corresponding Riesz projection cannot vanish. Hence the full torus
operator has an eigenvalue with a uniformly positive real part.

The localisation argument is performed on all vector fields because
cut-offs do not preserve the divergence constraint. At the end, taking the
divergence reduces the eigenvalue equation to a scalar
advection–diffusion equation. Its energy identity shows that an
eigenfunction withRe⁡λ>0\operatorname{Re}\lambda>0must in fact be divergence-free. The
non-zero axial Fourier index then allows us to take a real part without losing
the exact exponential norm law asserted in Theorem1.3.

## 1.4.Relation to earlier work

The local unstable model is based on Ponomarenko’s cylindrical screw flow,
which combines rotation about the cylinder axis with translation along it[40]. Gilbert analysed the high-conductivity limit of the
version with a jump at the cylinder boundary[25]. More
recently, Navarro-Fernández and Villringer constructed a rigorous
eigenvalue branch for a broad class of smooth helical profiles[37, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2]. Their magnetic eigenmodes concentrate in a
thin region around a distinguished radius, called a critical layer. Their
growth rate is of orderε1/3\varepsilon^{1/3}. In the present paper the discontinuous calculation
is used only to locate one isolated unstable eigenvalue. We then smooth the
velocity by norm-resolvent perturbation before using it in the global
construction.

Smooth autonomous fast dynamos are known on certain compact curved
three-manifolds[5,10], while the corresponding
smooth problem on the flat three-torus remains open. To our knowledge, the closest
autonomous Lipschitz result is the whole-space construction of Coti
Zelati, Sorella and Villringer[15, Theorem 1]. Recent rigorous
flat-torus constructions instead use time-dependent velocities and obtain
different forms of growth: growth for a prescribed countable set of
diffusivities[43, Theorem 1.5], growth only along a sequence of
times for every sufficiently small diffusivity[45, Theorem 1.1 and Section 6], or a
Floquet mode, meaning an eigenvector of the one-period solution operator[16, Theorem 1]. The present theorem supplies the
combination not covered by those works: one autonomous Lipschitz
velocity on the flat torus, persistent spectral growth, and every
sufficiently small diffusivity. The precise distinctions and
quantifiers are recorded in AppendixC.

## 1.5.Conclusion and outlook

The results in Theorem1.3and Corollary1.5separate the
long-time and zero-diffusivity regimes at Lipschitz regularity. The ideal
induction group has zero exponential growth, whereas the spectral growth
rates at positive diffusivity retain a uniform positive lower bound asε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0. This discrepancy arises from the multiscale construction:
the growing magnetic eigenmodes depend onε\varepsilonand move to successively
finer cells, rather than continuing a fixed unstable magnetic eigenmode of
the ideal equation.

The same multiscale feature explains the limitation of the construction.
It retains order-one gradients in shrinking cells accumulating at one axial
circle, so the velocity is notC1\mathrm{C}^{1}; a fixed mollification changes
precisely the cells matched to sufficiently small diffusivities. Thus the
example does not reach Conjecture1.2, and direct smoothing
does not preserve its mechanism. Resolving the smooth autonomous
fast-dynamo problem on𝕋3\mathbb{T}^{3}will require a different way of maintaining
uniform spectral instability across arbitrarily small diffusion scales.

## 1.6.Organisation of the paper

Section2constructs the smooth compactly supported local
velocity and proves the robust spectral statement used later.
Section3chooses the scales, places the cells, proves that
their sum is divergence-free and Lipschitz, and establishes the particle-flow
and zero-diffusivity conclusions in Proposition1.4.
Section4performs the axial Fourier reduction and proves exact
agreement with the matching local operator.
Section5establishes the small-cell tail estimate, the background
resolvent bound, the bounds for the models withm≠nm\neq n, and exponential decay across the
constant-coefficient buffer annuli. In Section6these
estimates produce the global
resolvent and a non-zero Riesz projection associated with the spectral contour.
Section7proves that the resulting complex eigenfunction is
divergence-free and hence is a magnetic eigenmode, extracts a real magnetic
field, and identifies itsH1\mathrm{H}^{1}-based magnetic length scale.
AppendixBrecords the energy
well-posedness used to identify the evolution generated by that magnetic
eigenmode with the unique solution
of (1.1); AppendixCcontains the extended
comparison with previous constructions.

Generative AI (OpenAI’s GPT-5.5 Pro and GPT-5.6 Sol) was used during
the exploratory stage of this work to help identify a candidate construction
of a fast dynamo. It was subsequently used to assist with and check
calculations and to identify errors, inconsistencies, and gaps in preliminary
versions of the arguments. It was also used to support the further development of some
arguments and the drafting and revision of parts of the exposition. All
mathematical claims, calculations, and AI-generated suggestions were
critically reviewed and verified by the author, who takes full responsibility
for the mathematical content and the final manuscript.

## 2.A compactly supported helical velocity with unstable spectrum

## 2.1.What the local construction must provide

This section constructs the single local unstable velocity profile later
copied into the torus.
Before stating the formal proposition at the end of this section, we explain what the construction must
provide and why each conclusion is needed later.

The local velocity field is the vector-valued mapv=(v⟂,v3):ℝ2→ℝ3v=(v_{\perp},v_{3})\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}. It has no dependence on the axial
variable:v⟂=(v1,v2)v_{\perp}=(v_{1},v_{2})gives the transverse motion andv3v_{3}gives the axial motion. Likewise,∇⟂\nabla_{\perp},Δ⟂\Delta_{\perp}, anddiv⟂\operatorname{div}_{\perp}act only on the
two variablesy=(y1,y2)y=(y_{1},y_{2}). For a non-zero axial wavenumberN∈2​π​(ℤ∖{0})N\in 2\pi(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}), we work in the fixed-NNx3x_{3}-Fourier subspace of fields proportional toei​N​x3\mathrm{e}^{iNx_{3}}, so
differentiation inx3x_{3}becomes multiplication byi​NiN. This is the
Fourier reduction used throughout the section.

The numberδ\deltais the rescaled diffusivity seen after a cell has been
rescaled to unit size. We need instability not merely at one value ofδ\delta, but on an intervalIIaround11; those overlapping
intervals will eventually cover every sufficiently small physical
diffusivity. A circleΓ\Gammain the right half-plane surrounds the
unstable eigenvalues. Its closed interior is denoted by𝒢\mathcal{G}. The reduced
operatorℒδ\mathcal{L}_{\delta}will be defined in
(2.17). Rather than label its individual eigenvalues, we
retain the Riesz projectionPδP_{\delta}, defined as the contour integral of
the resolvent aroundΓ\Gamma. Its range is the full generalised eigenspace
insideΓ\Gamma.
The projection varies continuously under smoothing and small changes ofδ\delta; consequently, the dimension of its range stays constant even
when an individual eigenvalue splits.

The final proposition states four conclusions used later.
The fixed contour and the non-zero range of its Riesz projection show that the
local instability persists uniformly inδ\delta; the divergence statement
recovers a divergence-free field for the original induction equation. TheL2\mathrm{L}^{2}-to-H2\mathrm{H}^{2}estimate controls
cut-off commutators. The spatial-localisation estimate says that one sufficiently
large disc contains all but an arbitrarily small fraction of theL2\mathrm{L}^{2}-mass of every unit vector in the unstable spectral subspaces; it
prevents the final localisation from cutting the instability away.
Throughout,𝟏E\mathbf{1}_{E}denotes multiplication by the indicator of a
measurable setEE.

We construct these objects in four stages. First, after introducing polar
coordinates, a discontinuous screw velocity reduces the equations for the radial
and angular componentsBrB_{r}andBθB_{\theta}to a scalar equation whose
zeros determine the eigenvalues; this is the dispersion relation. Second,
the divergence constraint supplies the axial component, and the transmission
conditions show that the resulting field belongs to the operator defined by
the weak form below. Third, convergence of the associated
forms allows us to smooth the velocity jump without losing the associated
spectral subspace. Finally, we extend the operator to all vector fields,
varyδ\deltanear11, and prove the two uniform estimates needed for
localisation. The last proof in the section collects these steps and
verifies each item of the proposition.

## 2.4.Approximation by a smooth velocity

the global construction requires a smooth local profile. Leth>0h>0denote the width of the annulus in which the jump is smoothed. Derivatives
of the smoothed profiles need
not remain bounded ash↓0h\downarrow 0, but the weak forms depend on the
velocity itself, whose difference fromV0V^{0}tends to zero inL4\mathrm{L}^{4}. This motivates the form formulation used for the smoothing.

For0<h<10<h<1, choose0≤χh≤10\leq\chi_{h}\leq 1inCc∞​([0,∞))\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{c}([0,\infty)), equal to one on[0,1−h][0,1-h]and zero on[1+h,∞)[1+h,\infty), and setvh​(y)=χh​(|y|)​(−Ω​y2,Ω​y1,U).v_{h}(y)=\chi_{h}(\left\lvert y\right\rvert)(-\Omega y_{2},\Omega y_{1},U).(2.11)

(2.2) by replacingV0V^{0}withvhv_{h}.

are bounded. Moreover,DN​𝒬N=Id,𝒫N2=𝒫N,Ran⁡𝒫N=HND_{N}\mathcal{Q}_{N}=\mathrm{Id},\qquad\mathcal{P}_{N}^{2}=\mathcal{P}_{N},\qquad\operatorname{Ran}\mathcal{P}_{N}=H_{N}

## Lemma 2.5(The unstable Riesz projection persists under smoothing).

There are a positively oriented circleΓ\Gamma, whose closed
interior lies in{Re⁡z>0}\{\operatorname{Re}z>0\}, andh0∈(0,1)h_{0}\in(0,1)such that for every0<h≤h00<h\leq h_{0}, the Riesz projection of the induction operatorAvhA_{v_{h}}associated withΓ\Gammahas finite, non-zero rank.
This rank equals the total algebraic multiplicity of the eigenvalues
inside the same circle forAV0A_{V^{0}}.

## Proof.

supported and divergence-free.

and the axial component is independent ofx3x_{3}.
Recall the operatorsDN,∇N,curlND_{N},\nabla_{N},\operatorname{curl}_{N}and the spaceHNH_{N}defined
above. For a bounded velocity fieldww, letAwA_{w}denote the induction
generator at unit diffusivity for the fixed-NNFourier subspace. On the common
form domainHN∩H1​(ℝ2;ℂ3)H_{N}\cap\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}), the closed sectorial form
associated with−Aw-A_{w}is𝔩w​(b,ϕ)=⟨∇Nb,∇Nϕ⟩−⟨w×b,curlN⁡ϕ⟩.\mathfrak{l}_{w}(b,\phi)=\left\langle\nabla_{N}b,\nabla_{N}\phi\right\rangle-\left\langle w\times b,\operatorname{curl}_{N}\phi\right\rangle.(2.13)

Using Hölder’s inequality with exponents4,4,24,4,2,|𝔩vh​(b,ϕ)−𝔩V0​(b,ϕ)|\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathfrak{l}_{v_{h}}(b,\phi)-\mathfrak{l}_{V^{0}}(b,\phi)\right\rvert≤‖vh−V0‖L4​‖b‖L4​‖curlN⁡ϕ‖2\displaystyle\leq\left\|v_{h}-V^{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}}\left\|b\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}}\left\|\operatorname{curl}_{N}\phi\right\|_{2}≤C​‖vh−V0‖L4​‖b‖H1​‖ϕ‖H1.\displaystyle\leq C\left\|v_{h}-V^{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}}\left\|b\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}}\left\|\phi\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}}.(2.14)

The second line uses the two-dimensional Sobolev embeddingH1​(ℝ2)↪L4​(ℝ2)\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
The coefficient difference is uniformly bounded and supported in an

## 2.5.Extension to all vector fields and uniform resolvent bounds

The later localisation argument multiplies resolvents by scalar cut-offs,
which do not preserve the divergence-free subspace. We therefore introduce
an operator on all vector fields. We extend the
transport–stretching expression, rather than the curl expression: the two
agree on divergence-free fields, while this extension has the exact
divergence identity used below.

ForB​(y,x3)=ei​N​x3​b​(y)B(y,x_{3})=\mathrm{e}^{iNx_{3}}b(y), withvvindependent ofx3x_{3},δ​Δ​B\displaystyle\delta\Delta B=ei​N​x3​δ​(Δ⟂−N2)​b,\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{iNx_{3}}\delta(\Delta_{\perp}-N^{2})b,−(v⋅∇)​B\displaystyle-(v\cdot\nabla)B=ei​N​x3​(−v⟂⋅∇⟂b−i​N​v3​b),\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{iNx_{3}}\bigl(-v_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}b-iNv_{3}b\bigr),(B⋅∇)​v\displaystyle(B\cdot\nabla)v=ei​N​x3​(b⟂⋅∇⟂)​v.\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{iNx_{3}}(b_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})v.

## Lemma 2.6(Comparison with the divergence-free restriction).

For everyδ>0\delta>0, in the open right half-planeℒδ\mathcal{L}_{\delta}and its
restriction toHNH_{N}have the same spectrum. Moreover, if a Riesz
contour and its closed interior lie in this half-plane, then the
corresponding Riesz projection ofℒδ\mathcal{L}_{\delta}has range inHNH_{N}and restricts to the divergence-free Riesz projection.

## Proof.

Taking the divergence sends the vector equation to a scalar
advection–diffusion equation. An energy estimate shows that this scalar
operator has no spectrum in the open right half-plane. We then use a
right inverse of the divergence map to split arbitrary data into a scalar
part and a divergence-free correction. This proves both directions of
the resolvent comparison.

PutΔN=Δ⟂−N2.\Delta_{N}=\Delta_{\perp}-N^{2}.

𝒢⊂{Re⁡z>0}\mathcal{G}\subset\{\operatorname{Re}z>0\};

for everyδ∈I=[δ−,δ+]\delta\in I=[\delta_{-},\delta_{+}],Γ⊂ρ​(ℒδ)\Gamma\subset\rho(\mathcal{L}_{\delta}), andPδP_{\delta}has the
same finite, non-zero rank;
- (iii)supδ∈Iz∈Γ‖(z−ℒδ)−1‖2→2<∞.\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta\in I\\
z\in\Gamma\end{subarray}}\left\|(z-\mathcal{L}_{\delta})^{-1}\right\|_{2\to 2}<\infty.

The rank in (ii) is constant becauseδ↦Pδ\delta\mapsto P_{\delta}is continuous in operator norm, and two
projections that are sufficiently close in norm have the same finite rank.
Joint norm-continuity of the resolvent on the compact setI×ΓI\times\Gammagives (iii), and the contour formula givessupδ∈I‖Pδ‖<∞.\sup_{\delta\in I}\left\|P_{\delta}\right\|<\infty.

It remains to upgrade theL2\mathrm{L}^{2}-resolvent bound to the statedH2\mathrm{H}^{2}-bound. The Fourier estimate forΔ⟂\Delta_{\perp}onℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2}, followed by interpolation to absorb the first-order terms,
gives uniformly‖b‖H2≤C​(‖(z−ℒδ)​b‖2+‖b‖2),b∈H2,δ∈I,z∈Γ.\left\|b\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{2}}\leq C\left(\left\|(z-\mathcal{L}_{\delta})b\right\|_{2}+\left\|b\right\|_{2}\right),\qquad b\in\mathrm{H}^{2},\quad\delta\in I,\quad z\in\Gamma.

Applying this estimate tob=(z−ℒδ)−1​fb=(z-\mathcal{L}_{\delta})^{-1}fand using (iii) proves
(2.20).
∎

A second consequence of the norm-continuity of the family of Riesz
projections{Pδ:δ∈I}\{P_{\delta}:\delta\in I\}, also needed globally, is
uniform spatial localisation. Concretely, this means that
every unit vector in the ranges ofPδP_{\delta}has arbitrarily little mass
outside one sufficiently large disc, uniformly inδ\delta.

## Lemma 2.8(Uniform spatial localisation of the spectral subspaces).

We havelimR→∞supδ∈I‖𝟏{|y|>R}​Pδ‖2→2=0.\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{\delta\in I}\left\|\mathbf{1}_{\{\left\lvert y\right\rvert>R\}}P_{\delta}\right\|_{2\to 2}=0.(2.21)

## Proof.

The mapδ↦Pδ\delta\mapsto P_{\delta}is norm-continuous on the compact intervalII, and everyPδP_{\delta}has finite rank. Thus{Pδ:δ∈I}\{P_{\delta}:\delta\in I\}is a norm-compact family of compact operators.
The multipliersMR=𝟏{|y|>R}M_{R}=\mathbf{1}_{\{\left\lvert y\right\rvert>R\}}

converge strongly to zero and satisfy‖MR‖≤1\left\|M_{R}\right\|\leq 1. Hence‖MR​P‖→0\left\|M_{R}P\right\|\to 0for every compact operatorPP, uniformly whenPPranges over a norm-compact set. Applying this observation to the
norm-compact family of Riesz projections{Pδ:δ∈I}\{P_{\delta}:\delta\in I\}proves
(2.21).

∎

The preceding results give the following local construction.

Lemmas2.1and2.2give an eigenvalue of

The remainder of the argument does not use the internal Bessel-function

## 3.Construction of the global velocity on the torus

## 3.1.Guide to the construction

We first introduce the notation; the formulas are given in the next two
subsections. By thenn-thcellwe mean one rescaled copy of the
local velocity field together with the larger transverse disc reserved
around it. The copy is assigned an integerKnK_{n}; the corresponding
fixed-KnK_{n}Fourier subspace consists of fields of the formB​(y,x3)=e2​π​i​Kn​x3​b​(y).B(y,x_{3})=\mathrm{e}^{2\pi iK_{n}x_{3}}b(y).

The local velocity field varies on the transverse scaleℓn=k∗/Kn\ell_{n}=k_{*}/K_{n}, and its support will be called thelocal-flow core.
The surrounding disc has radiusRn=ℓnR_{n}=\sqrt{\ell_{n}}and will be called
thebuffer. The buffer contains no additional local flow; it is
reserved for the cut-off and resolvent estimates in
Section5.

The objects are chosen in the following order. First we choose the
ratio of successive Fourier indices and the corresponding overlapping
diffusivity intervals.
Next we place the buffer discs disjointly and insert the rescaled local
flows. Finally we add a small axial velocity in each disc. It is
constant near the local-flow core and is smoothly reduced to zero only near
the outer edge of the buffer. These added velocities separate the spectra of different
cells, while the change over the larger buffer scale preserves the
Lipschitz bound. The sum of all these pieces is the desired velocity
field.

Only the velocity field is constructed in this section; no cell is yet
matched to a diffusivity. Givenε\varepsilon, Section4will select
an indexnnfor
whichε≍ℓn2\varepsilon\asymp\ell_{n}^{2}and use fieldse2​π​i​Kn​x3​b​(y)\mathrm{e}^{2\pi iK_{n}x_{3}}b(y).
Section5will then use the radiiRnR_{n}to prevent that
cell from interacting significantly with the others. Thus the core
scales are chosen for exact scaling to the local model, the buffer scales for spatial
localisation, and the added axial velocities for spectral separation.

Throughout the construction, the indicesm,nm,nrange overℤ≥0\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.
The local dataN=2​π​k∗,v,I,Γ,𝒢,g∗N=2\pi k_{*},\qquad v,\qquad I,\qquad\Gamma,\qquad\mathcal{G},\qquad g_{*}

this will also show that its distributional divergence vanishes. The local

support areaO​(Rn2)=O​(ℓn)O(R_{n}^{2})=O(\ell_{n}). Thus‖unloc‖1≤C​ℓn3,‖unax‖1≤C​ℓn2.\left\|u_{n}^{\rm loc}\right\|_{1}\leq C\ell_{n}^{3},\qquad\left\|u_{n}^{\rm ax}\right\|_{1}\leq C\ell_{n}^{2}.

Likewise,∇unloc\nabla u_{n}^{\rm loc}has sizeO​(1)O(1)on anO​(ℓn2)O(\ell_{n}^{2})set, whereas∇unax\nabla u_{n}^{\rm ax}has sizeO​(an/Rn)=O​(ℓn1/2)O(a_{n}/R_{n})=O(\ell_{n}^{1/2})on anO​(Rn2)=O​(ℓn)O(R_{n}^{2})=O(\ell_{n})set. Hence‖∇unloc‖1≤C​ℓn2,‖∇unax‖1≤C​ℓn3/2.\left\|\nabla u_{n}^{\rm loc}\right\|_{1}\leq C\ell_{n}^{2},\qquad\left\|\nabla u_{n}^{\rm ax}\right\|_{1}\leq C\ell_{n}^{3/2}.

Geometric decay ofℓn\ell_{n}makes all four bounds summable innn.

Letwn=unloc+unaxw_{n}=u_{n}^{\rm loc}+u_{n}^{\rm ax}. The displayed estimates give∑n≥0(‖wn‖1+‖∇wn‖1)<∞,\sum_{n\geq 0}\bigl(\left\|w_{n}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\nabla w_{n}\right\|_{1}\bigr)<\infty,

so the partial sums converge inW1,1\mathrm{W}^{1,1}. Absolute summability
permits termwise integration by parts against every smooth test function:∫𝕋2u​∂jϕ=∑n∫𝕋2wn​∂jϕ=−∑n∫𝕋2(∂jwn)​ϕ.\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}u\,\partial_{j}\phi=\sum_{n}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}w_{n}\,\partial_{j}\phi=-\sum_{n}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{j}w_{n})\phi.

Thus∂ju=∑n∂jwn\partial_{j}u=\sum_{n}\partial_{j}w_{n}distributionally, with no
additional measure atp∞p_{\infty}. Since the supports are disjoint,|∇u|≤‖∇v‖∞+ηaxalmost everywhere.\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert\leq\left\|\nabla v\right\|_{\infty}+\eta_{\rm ax}\quad\text{almost everywhere}.

Together with continuity, this provesu∈W1,∞u\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}. Summing
the corresponding divergence identities for the zero extensions of the
components givesdiv⁡u=0\operatorname{div}u=0in distributions.
∎

## 3.4.Particle dynamics and absence of exponential stretching

The particle dynamics are explicitly solvable: within each cell, a particle
keeps its distance from the cell centre, while its angular and axial
coordinates evolve at constant speeds depending only on that distance. Formula
(3.12) below gives the flow.

LetΦt:𝕋3→𝕋3\Phi_{t}:\mathbb{T}^{3}\to\mathbb{T}^{3}denote the unique particle flow generated byuu:dd​t​Φt​(x)=u​(Φt​(x)),Φ0​(x)=x.\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\Phi_{t}(x)=u(\Phi_{t}(x)),\qquad\Phi_{0}(x)=x.

Existence and uniqueness for everyt∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R}follow from the global
Lipschitz regularity in Lemma3.1. The special form of the
cells gives sharper information than the general exponential bound obtained
from Gronwall’s inequality.

## 3.5.Why the construction stops at Lipschitz regularity

The local velocity fieldvvis smooth. Regularity is lost only because the
global field has nonvanishing gradients at infinitely many shrinking
transverse scales. The estimate in the proof of
Lemma3.1shows thatuuis nevertheless differentiable
on the accumulation circle andD​u​(p∞,x3)=0(x3∈𝕋).Du(p_{\infty},x_{3})=0\qquad(x_{3}\in\mathbb{T}).

ChooseY0∈ℝ2Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}with∇⟂v​(Y0)≠0\nabla_{\perp}v(Y_{0})\neq 0; such a point exists because the non-zero
compactly supported fieldvvcannot have identically zero gradient.
Setyn=pn+ℓn​Y0.y_{n}=p_{n}+\ell_{n}Y_{0}.

For all sufficiently largenn,
(3.8) placesyny_{n}inside the region where
the added axial velocity is constant. The field is smooth nearyny_{n}, and∇⟂u​(yn,x3)=∇⟂v​(Y0),∂3u=0.\nabla_{\perp}u(y_{n},x_{3})=\nabla_{\perp}v(Y_{0}),\qquad\partial_{3}u=0.

Sinceyn→p∞y_{n}\to p_{\infty}while∇⟂v​(Y0)≠0\nabla_{\perp}v(Y_{0})\neq 0, the classical derivativeD​uDuis
discontinuous at every point of{p∞}×𝕋\{p_{\infty}\}\times\mathbb{T}.

The same observation rules out a continuous representative of the weak
gradient. Indeed, choose a small open neighbourhoodO∋Y0O\ni Y_{0}, withO¯⊂D​(0,Rv)\overline{O}\subset D(0,R_{v}), on which∇⟂v\nabla_{\perp}vstays
separated from zero. On every rescaled open setpn+ℓn​Op_{n}+\ell_{n}O, the weak gradient agrees almost everywhere with the
corresponding smooth rescaling, whereas it vanishes almost everywhere on
open gaps between the buffer discs. A continuous representative would
therefore have two incompatible limits at the accumulation circle. Thus∇u\nabla uhas no continuous representative there andu∉C1u\notin\mathrm{C}^{1},
even thoughuuis differentiable everywhere.

Nor can we smooth this particular construction at one fixed length scale
without changing its small-diffusivity mechanism. A fixed mollification
alters every local-flow core below the mollification length, and the exact
scaling to the local spectral problem is then lost on the
tail of cells needed asε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0. This observation does not
exclude a different instability mechanism for the mollified velocity.
Rather, it identifies the obstruction faced by the present multiscale
strategy: instability must persist at arbitrarily small resistive length
scales while the velocity gradients become compatible at their accumulation
set. Here a resistive length scale is a magnetic-field scale at which
diffusion balances induction; see[35].

## 4.Axial Fourier reduction and scaling to the local model

This section gives the algebra underlying the scale choice. Onceε\varepsilonis given, we select a cell whose core size satisfiesε/ℓn2∈I\varepsilon/\ell_{n}^{2}\in Iand work in the fixed-KnK_{n}Fourier subspace.
Translation to the centre of that cell and rescaling byℓn\ell_{n}turn
the whole-plane local operator—which retains the local copy and
extends the constant axial valuean​e3a_{n}e_{3}to all ofℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2}—into the fixed local operator from
Section2, up to the scalar term−i​τ-i\tau. This term
translates the spectrum vertically and therefore leaves every spectral
real part unchanged. The agreement is exact; estimates are needed only to
compare this isolated cell with the full torus operator.

As in the local analysis, the cut-off construction is carried out on all
vector fields because multiplication by a spatial cut-off does not preserve
the divergence constraint. The eigenfunction obtained at the end will be
shown to be divergence-free. As before, writeu=(u⟂,u3)u=(u_{\perp},u_{3})andb=(b⟂,b3)b=(b_{\perp},b_{3})for the transverse and axial
components. Define the torus operator on all vector fields by𝒜ε​B=ε​Δ​B−(u⋅∇)​B+(B⋅∇)​u,D​(𝒜ε)=H2​(𝕋3;ℂ3).\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}B=\varepsilon\Delta B-(u\cdot\nabla)B+(B\cdot\nabla)u,\qquad D(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})=\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}).

On divergence-free fields,𝒜ε\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}agrees withcurl(u×⋅)+εΔ\operatorname{curl}(u\times\cdot)+\varepsilon\Delta. Becauseuuis independent ofx3x_{3},

of the Laplacian becomes−(2​π​K)2-(2\pi K)^{2}, and(b⋅∇)​u=(b⟂⋅∇⟂)​u(b\cdot\nabla)u=(b_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})ubecause∂3u=0\partial_{3}u=0. Thus, onL2​(𝕋2;ℂ3)\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}), defineAε,K​b=\displaystyle A_{\varepsilon,K}b={}ε​(Δ⟂−(2​π​K)2)​b−u⟂⋅∇⟂b−2​π​i​K​u3​b\displaystyle\varepsilon\bigl(\Delta_{\perp}-(2\pi K)^{2}\bigr)b-u_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}b-2\pi iKu_{3}b+(b⟂⋅∇⟂)​u,D​(Aε,K)=H2​(𝕋2;ℂ3).\displaystyle+(b_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})u,\qquad D(A_{\varepsilon,K})=\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}).(4.1)

## Lemma 4.1(Basic properties of the fixed-KKoperator).

For everyε>0\varepsilon>0andK∈ℤK\in\mathbb{Z}, the operatorAε,KA_{\varepsilon,K}is
closed inL2​(𝕋2;ℂ3)\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}), with domainH2​(𝕋2;ℂ3)\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}), and its resolvent set is nonempty. For one, and
hence every,z∈ρ​(Aε,K)z\in\rho(A_{\varepsilon,K}), the resolvent(z−Aε,K)−1(z-A_{\varepsilon,K})^{-1}is compact.

## Proof.

The first-order terms areΔ⟂\Delta_{\perp}-bounded with arbitrarily small
relative bound: for everyη>0\eta>0, there is a constantCηC_{\eta}such that‖u⟂⋅∇⟂b‖2≤η​‖Δ⟂​b‖2+Cη​‖b‖2,b∈H2​(𝕋2;ℂ3).\left\|u_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}b\right\|_{2}\leq\eta\left\|\Delta_{\perp}b\right\|_{2}+C_{\eta}\left\|b\right\|_{2},\qquad b\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}).

This follows from the standard interpolation estimate.
The remaining coefficients are bounded. The standard perturbation theorem[29, Theorem IV.1.1]therefore gives closedness and equivalence of
the graph andH2\mathrm{H}^{2}-norms.

It remains to verify that the resolvent set is not empty. Forb,ϕ∈H1​(𝕋2;ℂ3)b,\phi\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}), the weak form of(z−Aε,K)​b=f(z-A_{\varepsilon,K})b=fis𝔞z,K​(b,ϕ):=\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{z,K}(b,\phi):={}z​⟨b,ϕ⟩+ε​⟨∇⟂b,∇⟂ϕ⟩+ε​(2​π​K)2​⟨b,ϕ⟩\displaystyle z\left\langle b,\phi\right\rangle+\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla_{\perp}b,\nabla_{\perp}\phi\right\rangle+\varepsilon(2\pi K)^{2}\left\langle b,\phi\right\rangle+⟨u⟂⋅∇⟂b,ϕ⟩+2​π​i​K​⟨u3​b,ϕ⟩−⟨(b⟂⋅∇⟂)​u,ϕ⟩=⟨f,ϕ⟩.\displaystyle+\left\langle u_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}b,\phi\right\rangle+2\pi iK\left\langle u_{3}b,\phi\right\rangle-\left\langle(b_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})u,\phi\right\rangle=\left\langle f,\phi\right\rangle.

Sincediv⟂⁡u⟂=0\operatorname{div}_{\perp}u_{\perp}=0, transverse transport has zero real
part. Sinceu3u_{3}is real, the axial term is purely imaginary.
ThereforeRe⁡𝔞z,K​(b,b)≥(Re⁡z−‖∇u‖∞)​‖b‖22+ε​‖∇⟂b‖22+ε​(2​π​K)2​‖b‖22.\operatorname{Re}\mathfrak{a}_{z,K}(b,b)\geq\bigl(\operatorname{Re}z-\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\infty}\bigr)\left\|b\right\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{\perp}b\right\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon(2\pi K)^{2}\left\|b\right\|_{2}^{2}.

The form is coercive whenRe⁡z>‖∇u‖∞+1\operatorname{Re}z>\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\infty}+1, and Lax–Milgram gives a unique weakH1\mathrm{H}^{1}-solution for everyL2\mathrm{L}^{2}datum. Rearranging the equation
givesε​Δ⟂​b=\displaystyle\varepsilon\Delta_{\perp}b={}z​b+ε​(2​π​K)2​b+u⟂⋅∇⟂b+2​π​i​K​u3​b\displaystyle zb+\varepsilon(2\pi K)^{2}b+u_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}b+2\pi iKu_{3}b−(b⟂⋅∇⟂)​u−f,\displaystyle-(b_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})u-f,

whose right-hand side belongs toL2\mathrm{L}^{2}. Periodic elliptic regularity
givesb∈H2b\in\mathrm{H}^{2}. Hence suchzzbelongs to the resolvent set.

Thus the resolvent mapsL2\mathrm{L}^{2}intoH2\mathrm{H}^{2}; Rellich compactness ofH2​(𝕋2)↪L2​(𝕋2)\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2})makes it compact.
∎

## 5.Uniform localisation estimates

This section prepares three estimates for the gluing argument. Their
roles are different. First, after finitely many cells have been removed,
all still smaller cells occupy so little area that their combined
stretching is small in the energy form. The local flows withm>n+Jm>n+Jmay therefore remain in the background operator instead of being treated
by separate whole-plane resolvents. Second, each of the finitely many
remaining cells withm≠nm\neq nhas a uniformly bounded resolvent near the
chosen spectral contour; the added axial velocities are used only for this
separation. Third, a solution of the constant-coefficient resolvent
equation decays exponentially while crossing a constant-coefficient
buffer. This last estimate makes the errors caused by spatial cut-offs
small.

All three bounds must be uniform in the matching indexnn, sincen→∞n\to\inftyasε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0. They are combined only in
Section6.
Throughout this section,ε\varepsilonandnnsatisfy0<ε≤ε0,δn=εℓn2∈I.0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0},\qquad\delta_{n}=\frac{\varepsilon}{\ell_{n}^{2}}\in I.

The estimates below are conditional onε0≤1\varepsilon_{0}\leq 1andηax<g∗/8\eta_{\rm ax}<g_{*}/8. Afterτ\tauis fixed in
Lemma5.4, enlarge the preliminary thresholdKpreK_{\rm pre}, if necessary, so that both conditions hold for everyK0≥KpreK_{0}\geq K_{\rm pre}. This does not alter any earlier choice, and the
final enlargement ofK0K_{0}in Section6preserves both
conditions.

The four operators used in this section differ only in which cell
coefficients they retain. The following table summarises them; each operator
is defined by a formula before it enters an estimate.OperatorSpaceVelocity retainedAε,KnA_{\varepsilon,K_{n}}𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}all cellsAε,nbgA^{\rm bg}_{\varepsilon,n}𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}every smoothly cut off added axial velocityumaxu_{m}^{\rm ax}, and local flows withm>n+Jm>n+JAε,n(m)A^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}ℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2}all three components ofumlocu_{m}^{\rm loc}, together with the constant axial valueam​e3a_{m}e_{3}A^ε,n(m)\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}ℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2}all three components ofumlocu_{m}^{\rm loc}, but without the constant axial valueam​e3a_{m}e_{3}

## 5.1.Energy estimate for the small-cell tail

The gradients of the rescaled local velocities are uniformly bounded but do
not decrease with the scale. The contribution of the infinitely many terms
withm>n+Jm>n+Jis small because of the total area of their supports, rather than anL∞\mathrm{L}^{\infty}bound. More precisely, the stretching contribution is bounded by a small multiple of the natural
energy‖f‖22+ε​‖∇⟂f‖22\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}^{2}. Transport does not
enter this real-energy bound because it is skew on the torus.

For an integerJ≥0J\geq 0, setΩn,Jtail=⋃m>n+Jsupp​∇⟂umloc.\Omega^{\rm tail}_{n,J}=\bigcup_{m>n+J}\operatorname{supp}\nabla_{\perp}u_{m}^{\rm loc}.

Only the supports of these gradients enter because this lemma controls the
stretching term. The real part of the transport term vanishes by
incompressibility.

## Lemma 5.1(Small-cell tail estimate).

There is a sequenceθJ→0\theta_{J}\to 0, independent ofnnandε\varepsilon,
such that everyf∈H1​(𝕋2;ℂ3)f\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3})satisfies∫Ωn,Jtail|f|2≤θJ​(‖f‖22+ε​‖∇⟂f‖22).\int_{\Omega^{\rm tail}_{n,J}}\left\lvert f\right\rvert^{2}\leq\theta_{J}\left(\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}^{2}\right).(5.1)

## Proof.

These supports are pairwise disjoint, so integration overΩn,Jtail\Omega^{\rm tail}_{n,J}is
the sum of the corresponding local integrals.
Sincesupp​∇⟂umloc\operatorname{supp}\nabla_{\perp}u_{m}^{\rm loc}has area at mostC​ℓm2C\ell_{m}^{2}, andℓm≤ℓn​q−(m−n)\ell_{m}\leq\ell_{n}q^{-(m-n)},|Ωn,Jtail|\displaystyle\left\lvert\Omega^{\rm tail}_{n,J}\right\rvert≤C​∑m>n+Jℓm2≤C​ℓn2​∑r>Jq−2​r≤C​ℓn2​q−2​J,\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{m>n+J}\ell_{m}^{2}\leq C\ell_{n}^{2}\sum_{r>J}q^{-2r}\leq C\ell_{n}^{2}q^{-2J},|Ωn,Jtail|1/2\displaystyle\left\lvert\Omega^{\rm tail}_{n,J}\right\rvert^{1/2}≤C​ℓn​q−J≤C​ε​q−J.\displaystyle\leq C\ell_{n}q^{-J}\leq C\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,q^{-J}.(5.2)

Here the last inequality usesδn=ε/ℓn2∈[δ−,δ+]\delta_{n}=\varepsilon/\ell_{n}^{2}\in[\delta_{-},\delta_{+}].

The two-dimensional Ladyzhenskaya inequality on𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}yields‖f‖L4​(𝕋2)2≤C​‖f‖2​(‖f‖2+‖∇⟂f‖2).\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{2}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{2}\bigl(\left\|f\right\|_{2}+\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}\bigr).

Assumingε0≤1\varepsilon_{0}\leq 1, Hölder’s inequality,
(5.2), and Young’s inequality therefore give,
componentwise forf∈H1​(𝕋2;ℂ3)f\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}),∫Ωn,Jtail|f|2\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{\rm tail}_{n,J}}\left\lvert f\right\rvert^{2}≤|Ωn,Jtail|1/2​‖f‖L4​(𝕋2)2\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\Omega^{\rm tail}_{n,J}\right\rvert^{1/2}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}^{2}≤C​ε​q−J​‖f‖2​(‖f‖2+‖∇⟂f‖2)\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,q^{-J}\left\|f\right\|_{2}\bigl(\left\|f\right\|_{2}+\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}\bigr)≤C​q−J​(‖f‖22+ε​‖∇⟂f‖22).\displaystyle\leq Cq^{-J}\left(\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}^{2}\right).

In the last line we usedε≤1\varepsilon\leq 1andε​‖f‖2​‖∇⟂f‖2≤12​(‖f‖22+ε​‖∇⟂f‖22).\sqrt{\varepsilon}\left\|f\right\|_{2}\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}^{2}\right).

The result follows withθJ=C​q−J→0\theta_{J}=Cq^{-J}\to 0.

By density, the preceding coercive estimate extends fromH2\mathrm{H}^{2}toH1\mathrm{H}^{1}, so Lax–Milgram gives a unique weak solutiong∈H1g\in\mathrm{H}^{1}; this is the standard
form realisation of a sectorial operator[29, Theorem VI.2.1]. Rearranging the equation givesε​Δ⟂​g=\displaystyle\varepsilon\Delta_{\perp}g={}z​g+ε​(2​π​Kn)2​g+(un,Jbg)⟂⋅∇⟂g+2​π​i​Kn​(un,Jbg)3​g\displaystyle zg+\varepsilon(2\pi K_{n})^{2}g+(u^{\rm bg}_{n,J})_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}g+2\pi iK_{n}(u^{\rm bg}_{n,J})_{3}g−(g⟂⋅∇⟂)​un,Jbg−f.\displaystyle-(g_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})u^{\rm bg}_{n,J}-f.

Its right-hand side belongs toL2\mathrm{L}^{2}: the velocity field is bounded,∇⟂g∈L2\nabla_{\perp}g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}, and all stretching coefficients are bounded.
Fourier-series elliptic regularity on𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}yieldsg∈H2g\in\mathrm{H}^{2}.
Hence this weak solution lies in the stated
operator domain and proves surjectivity.

Applying the coercive estimate to this solution givesg∗4​‖g‖22+34​ε​‖∇⟂g‖22≤‖f‖2​‖g‖2,\frac{g_{*}}{4}\left\|g\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{3}{4}\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{\perp}g\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{2}\left\|g\right\|_{2},

and therefore‖g‖2≤4g∗​‖f‖2,ε​‖∇⟂g‖22≤C​‖f‖22.\left\|g\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{4}{g_{*}}\left\|f\right\|_{2},\qquad\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{\perp}g\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}.

These estimates prove both invertibility and (5.3),
uniformly forRe⁡z≥g∗/2\operatorname{Re}z\geq g_{*}/2.
∎

We fix this value ofJJfor the remainder of the proof.

## 5.3.Resolvent bounds for the local models

Only them=nm=noperator is reduced to the fixed local spectral family.
Form≠nm\neq n, the rescaled diffusivity and axial wavenumber are
different, so we do not require detailed spectral information. Instead, we
use an energy estimate for the real part and the added axial velocities to
separate the numerical ranges in the imaginary direction.

For a closed operatorTT, its numerical range isW​(T):={⟨T​f,f⟩:f∈D​(T),‖f‖2=1}.W(T):=\{\left\langle Tf,f\right\rangle:f\in D(T),\ \left\|f\right\|_{2}=1\}.

It is a coarse enclosure of the spectrum, but distance from bothW​(T)W(T)andW​(T∗)W(T^{*})gives a quantitative resolvent bound.

Adding the constant axial value givesAε,n(m)=A^ε,n(m)−2​π​i​Kn​am.A^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}=\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}-2\pi iK_{n}a_{m}.

Define the matrixGm​(y)∈ℝ3×3G_{m}(y)\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}by(Gm​(y)​ξ)i=∑j=12∂j(umloc)i​(y)​ξj,i=1,2,3.(G_{m}(y)\xi)_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\partial_{j}(u_{m}^{\rm loc})_{i}(y)\,\xi_{j},\qquad i=1,2,3.

ThusGm​b=(b⟂⋅∇⟂)​umlocG_{m}b=(b_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})u_{m}^{\rm loc}. For fixedε,m,n\varepsilon,m,n, writeA^ε,n(m)=T0+ℬm,n,T0=ε​(Δ⟂−(2​π​Kn)2),\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}=T_{0}+\mathcal{B}_{m,n},\qquad T_{0}=\varepsilon\bigl(\Delta_{\perp}-(2\pi K_{n})^{2}\bigr),

In other words, for everyη>0\eta>0there isCηC_{\eta}, depending on the fixed triple(ε,m,n)(\varepsilon,m,n), such that‖ℬm,n​b‖2+‖ℬm,n†​b‖2≤η​‖T0​b‖2+Cη​‖b‖2,b∈H2.\left\|\mathcal{B}_{m,n}b\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathcal{B}_{m,n}^{\dagger}b\right\|_{2}\leq\eta\left\|T_{0}b\right\|_{2}+C_{\eta}\left\|b\right\|_{2},\qquad b\in\mathrm{H}^{2}.

## Lemma 5.3(Numerical range before the constant axial shift).

There is a constantCnumC_{\rm num}, independent ofm,n,εm,n,\varepsilon,
such that, wheneverf∈H2f\in\mathrm{H}^{2},‖f‖2=1\left\|f\right\|_{2}=1, andRe⁡⟨A^ε,n(m)​f,f⟩≥g∗/2,\operatorname{Re}\left\langle\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}f,f\right\rangle\geq g_{*}/2,

we haveε​‖∇⟂f‖2≤Cnum,|Im⁡⟨A^ε,n(m)​f,f⟩|≤Cnum​(1+KnKm).\sqrt{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}\leq C_{\rm num},\qquad\left\lvert\operatorname{Im}\left\langle\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}f,f\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq C_{\rm num}\left(1+\frac{K_{n}}{K_{m}}\right).

The analogous implication holds for(A^ε,n(m))∗(\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n})^{*}, with the same constant after one
enlargement.

## Proof.

Taking the real part of theL2\mathrm{L}^{2}inner-product identity givesRe⁡⟨A^ε,n(m)​f,f⟩=\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\left\langle\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}f,f\right\rangle={}−ε​‖∇⟂f‖22−ε​(2​π​Kn)2\displaystyle-\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}^{2}-\varepsilon(2\pi K_{n})^{2}+Re⁡⟨(f⟂⋅∇⟂)​umloc,f⟩.\displaystyle+\operatorname{Re}\left\langle(f_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})u_{m}^{\rm loc},f\right\rangle.

The transport terms have zero real part, and the last term is bounded by‖∇umloc‖∞\left\|\nabla u_{m}^{\rm loc}\right\|_{\infty}. Discarding the nonpositive axial
diffusion term, the hypothesis of the lemma givesg∗2≤−ε​‖∇⟂f‖22+‖∇umloc‖∞.\frac{g_{*}}{2}\leq-\varepsilon\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\nabla u_{m}^{\rm loc}\right\|_{\infty}.

It follows, with a constantC0C_{0}independent ofm,n,εm,n,\varepsilon, thatε​‖∇⟂f‖2≤C0.\sqrt{\varepsilon}\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}\leq C_{0}.

Since‖umloc‖∞≤C​ℓm\left\|u_{m}^{\rm loc}\right\|_{\infty}\leq C\ell_{m}andKn​ε=k∗​δn,K_{n}\sqrt{\varepsilon}=k_{*}\sqrt{\delta_{n}},

we may chooseCnum≥C0C_{\rm num}\geq C_{0}, independently ofm,n,εm,n,\varepsilon, so that, under the same hypotheses,|Im⁡⟨A^ε,n(m)​f,f⟩|≤Cnum​(1+KnKm).\left\lvert\operatorname{Im}\left\langle\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}f,f\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq C_{\rm num}\left(1+\frac{K_{n}}{K_{m}}\right).(5.5)

Indeed, the transverse transport, axial transport, and stretching
contributions to the imaginary part satisfy|Im⁡⟨A^ε,n(m)​f,f⟩|\displaystyle\left\lvert\operatorname{Im}\left\langle\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n}f,f\right\rangle\right\rvert≤C​ℓm​‖∇⟂f‖2+C​Kn​ℓm+C\displaystyle\leq C\ell_{m}\left\|\nabla_{\perp}f\right\|_{2}+CK_{n}\ell_{m}+C≤C​(ℓmε+Kn​ℓm+1)\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{\ell_{m}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+K_{n}\ell_{m}+1\right)≤C​(1+KnKm),\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\frac{K_{n}}{K_{m}}\right),

whereℓmε=1δn​KnKm,Kn​ℓm=k∗​KnKm.\frac{\ell_{m}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta_{n}}}\frac{K_{n}}{K_{m}},\qquad K_{n}\ell_{m}=k_{*}\frac{K_{n}}{K_{m}}.

In the displayed formula forℬm,n†\mathcal{B}_{m,n}^{\dagger}, the two
transport signs are reversed andGmG_{m}is replaced byGm𝖳G_{m}^{\mathsf{T}}. These changes preserve all three absolute-value
bounds above, so the same implication holds for(A^ε,n(m))∗(\widehat{A}^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n})^{*}, with the same value ofCnumC_{\rm num}after enlarging it once.

Otherwise (5.5) applies, andIm⁡(z−ν)=Im⁡ζ−Im⁡ν^+2​π​Kn​(am−an).\operatorname{Im}(z-\nu)=\operatorname{Im}\zeta-\operatorname{Im}\widehat{\nu}+2\pi K_{n}(a_{m}-a_{n}).

## 5.4.Exponential decay for the constant-coefficient equation

In a buffer the local flow has vanished and the added axial velocity is
constant.
For example, in the fixed-KnK_{n}reduction and within the constant axial region of themm-th cell,
the local operator isε​(Δ⟂−(2​π​Kn)2)−2​π​i​Kn​am.\varepsilon\bigl(\Delta_{\perp}-(2\pi K_{n})^{2}\bigr)-2\pi iK_{n}a_{m}.

Consequently, the homogeneous equation(z−Aε,n(m))​g=0(z-A^{(m)}_{\varepsilon,n})g=0reduces to(−ε​Δ⟂+μm,n​(z))​g=0,μm,n​(z)=z+ε​(2​π​Kn)2+2​π​i​Kn​am(-\varepsilon\Delta_{\perp}+\mu_{m,n}(z))g=0,\qquad\mu_{m,n}(z)=z+\varepsilon(2\pi K_{n})^{2}+2\pi iK_{n}a_{m}

componentwise.
In particular,Re⁡μm,n​(z)=Re⁡z+ε​(2​π​Kn)2\operatorname{Re}\mu_{m,n}(z)=\operatorname{Re}z+\varepsilon(2\pi K_{n})^{2}, independently
of the constant axial value. We use decay in both
directions. A solution of the background resolvent equation has no forcing near an omitted core and
must be small when it reaches that core; a local whole-plane solution is
forced near the core and must be small when it reaches the outer cut-off.

Forg∈H1​(𝒪;ℂ3)g\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{C}^{3}), where𝒪⊂ℝ2\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}is open, use the
scaled norm‖g‖Hε1​(𝒪)=‖g‖L2​(𝒪)+ε​‖∇⟂g‖L2​(𝒪).\left\|g\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O})}=\left\|g\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O})}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\left\|\nabla_{\perp}g\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O})}.

The positive real part of the zeroth-order coefficient in(−ε​Δ⟂+μ)​g=0(-\varepsilon\Delta_{\perp}+\mu)g=0makes solutions decay over distances
comparable toε\sqrt{\varepsilon}. The following weighted estimate is uniform in
the imaginary part ofμ\mu.

## Lemma 5.7(Interior and exterior exponential decay).

then‖g‖Hε1​(D​(p,R/10))≤C​exp⁡(−c​Rε)​‖g‖Hε1​(D​(p,R/5)).\left\|g\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(D(p,R/10))}\leq C\exp\left(-c\frac{R}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\left\|g\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(D(p,R/5))}.(5.10)

then‖g‖Hε1​(ℝ2∖D​(p,R/3))≤C​exp⁡(−c​Rε)​‖g‖Hε1​(ℝ2∖D​(p,R/4)).\left\|g\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus D(p,R/3))}\leq C\exp\left(-c\frac{R}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\left\|g\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus D(p,R/4))}.(5.11)

IfR<εR<\sqrt{\varepsilon}, then the exponential factor is bounded below bye−c\mathrm{e}^{-c}, so both conclusions follow from restriction of theHε1\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\varepsilon}-norm after increasingCC. We may therefore assumeR≥εR\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Interior estimate.Setr=|y−p|r=\left\lvert y-p\right\rvertands+:=max⁡{s,0}s_{+}:=\max\{s,0\}. ## 6.A global approximate inverse and an unstable eigenvalue

The preceding sections have reduced the proof to one comparison. At the
fixed Fourier indexKnK_{n}, the whole-plane model for the matching cell has
a non-zero Riesz projection associated withΓτ\Gamma_{\tau}; we must show that the full torus
operator has one as well. The argument has three steps. We first join the
background and local resolvents and compute the
error of this approximate inverse. We then make that error small and
deduce that the full resolvent exists on the contour. Finally, we compare
the contour integrals of the approximate and true resolvents and show that
the latter cannot vanish. These steps are recorded as separate lemmas and
then combined in the proposition.

## Proposition 6.1(Existence of an unstable torus eigenvalue).

The initial indexK0K_{0}can be chosen, afterq,q¯,J,τq,\bar{q},J,\tau, so that
for every0<ε≤ε00<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}and everynnsatisfyingδn=ε/ℓn2∈I\delta_{n}=\varepsilon/\ell_{n}^{2}\in I,Γτ⊂ρ​(Aε,Kn)\Gamma_{\tau}\subset\rho(A_{\varepsilon,K_{n}})

and the Riesz projectionQε,n=12​π​i​∫Γτ(z−Aε,Kn)−1​dzQ_{\varepsilon,n}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{\tau}}(z-A_{\varepsilon,K_{n}})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}z

is non-zero. ConsequentlyAε,KnA_{\varepsilon,K_{n}}has an eigenvalue in𝒢τ\mathcal{G}_{\tau}, whose real part is at leastg∗g_{*}.

We now construct the approximate inverse, or parametrix. It glues the
background resolvent, which retains the remaining smaller cells, to finitely
many whole-plane cell resolvents. The exact identity in
Lemma6.2leaves only the error caused by the local
velocities omitted from the background operator and the cut-off commutators.
Both are controlled by the decay estimates of the preceding
section.

## 6.1.The approximate inverse

For the remainder of this section, fixε\varepsilonandnnsatisfying0<ε≤ε00<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}andδn=ε/ℓn2∈I\delta_{n}=\varepsilon/\ell_{n}^{2}\in I.
When uniformity matters, we writeεn,δ=δ​ℓn2\varepsilon_{n,\delta}=\delta\ell_{n}^{2},δ∈I\delta\in I, and attach the
subscript(n,δ)(n,\delta)to the corresponding background operator,
parametrix, and error. For the fixed pair,δ=δn\delta=\delta_{n}, we use the
shorter notation below. Thus the symbolsℛapp\mathcal{R}_{\rm app},EnE_{n}, and
the cut-offs all retain their dependence onε\varepsilonandnn; it is
suppressed only to keep the formulas readable.

Two cut-offs are attached to each cell. The factorϕm\phi_{m}localises the
resolvent datum near themm-th core before the whole-plane local resolvent
is applied. The larger cut-offψm\psi_{m}cuts off the resulting solution
before it reaches the region where the local model and the torus coefficients
cease to agree. The gap between their transition regions is where exponential
decay is gained.

Choose real-valued radial cut-offsϕm,ψm∈Cc∞​(ℝ2)\phi_{m},\psi_{m}\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),0≤ϕm,ψm≤10\leq\phi_{m},\psi_{m}\leq 1,

## Remark 6.6(The Riesz-projection comparison does not localise individual
reduced eigenvectors).

The conclusion concerns spectral subspaces. The comparison uses the
local Riesz projection to prove that the global Riesz projection
is non-zero. Without an additional
simplicity statement for the spectrum inside the contour, it does not by
itself give quantitative localisation of an individual global
reduced eigenvector.

Order of the parameter choices.The parameters are chosen as follows. The local dataN,v,I,Γ,𝒢,g∗N,v,I,\Gamma,\mathcal{G},g_{*}are fixed first. We then choose1<q<q¯<δ+/δ−1<q<\bar{q}<\sqrt{\delta_{+}/\delta_{-}}and a preliminary thresholdKpreK_{\rm pre}. NextJJis fixed by
Proposition5.2, andτ\tauby
Lemma5.4; neither depends onK0K_{0}. Onceτ\tauis fixed, enlargeKpreK_{\rm pre}, if necessary, so thatε0≤1\varepsilon_{0}\leq 1and (3.7) hold for everyK0≥KpreK_{0}\geq K_{\rm pre}, as required at the start of
Section5. Finally choose oneK0≥KpreK_{0}\geq K_{\rm pre}large
enough that (3.4),
(3.8), (6.11), and
(6.16) all hold. Each requirement is preserved, or
improves, whenK0K_{0}is increased, and all constants entering them are
uniform after the preceding choices. This fixes the sequencesKn,ℓn,RnK_{n},\ell_{n},R_{n}, the velocityuu, andε0\varepsilon_{0};
only the matching indexn=n​(ε)n=n(\varepsilon)subsequently depends on the
diffusivity.

## Proof of Proposition6.1.

Lemmas6.2and6.3give the true
torus resolvent onΓτ\Gamma_{\tau}. By
Lemma6.5, its Riesz projection is non-zero and
hence the contour encloses an eigenvalue. Vertical translation by−i​τ-i\taudoes not change real parts, so every point of𝒢τ\mathcal{G}_{\tau}has real part at leastg∗g_{*}. This proves all assertions
of the proposition.
∎

## 7.Divergence-free and real-valued solutions; proof of the main theorem

The cut-off argument was carried out on complex-valued vector fields without
imposing the divergence constraint, because multiplication by a cut-off does
not preserve that constraint. It remains to show that positive growth forces
the eigenfunction to be divergence-free and that the non-zero axial Fourier
index lets us take a real part without losing its norm.

## 7.1.The eigenfunction is divergence-free

Recall that the operator on all vector fields is𝒜ε​V=ε​Δ​V−(u⋅∇)​V+(V⋅∇)​u.\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}V=\varepsilon\Delta V-(u\cdot\nabla)V+(V\cdot\nabla)u.

On divergence-free fields it agrees withLε,uL_{\varepsilon,u}.

A similar divergence argument for magnetic eigenmodes of helical flows appears in[37, Lemma 4.4]; see also[16, Proof of Theorem 1, Step 3]for a Floquet mode.

## Lemma 7.1(Eigenfunctions withRe⁡λ>0\operatorname{Re}\lambda>0are divergence-free).

Letu∈W1,∞​(𝕋3;ℝ3)u\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})be divergence-free. If0≠V∈H2​(𝕋3;ℂ3)0\neq V\in\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})satisfies𝒜ε​V=λ​V,Re⁡λ>0,\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}V=\lambda V,\qquad\operatorname{Re}\lambda>0,

thendiv⁡V=0\operatorname{div}V=0. ConsequentlyLε,u​V=λ​VL_{\varepsilon,u}V=\lambda V.

## Proof.

Setd:=div⁡Vd:=\operatorname{div}V. The general vector identity displayed in the
introduction, used before imposingd=0d=0, gives−(u⋅∇)​V+(V⋅∇)​u=curl⁡(u×V)−u​d,-(u\cdot\nabla)V+(V\cdot\nabla)u=\operatorname{curl}(u\times V)-u\,d,

becausediv⁡u=0\operatorname{div}u=0. All terms are well defined in distributions whenu∈W1,∞u\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}andV∈H2V\in\mathrm{H}^{2}. ## 7.2.Passage to a real-valued solution

## Lemma 7.2(Real part of a non-zerox3x_{3}-Fourier mode).

LetK∈ℤ∖{0}K\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\},b∈L2​(𝕋2;ℂ3)b\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{3}), andφ∈ℝ\varphi\in\mathbb{R}. Then, for almost everyyy,∫01|Re⁡(ei​φ​e2​π​i​K​x3​b​(y))|2​dx3=12​|b​(y)|ℂ32.\int_{0}^{1}\left\lvert\operatorname{Re}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{i\varphi}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi iKx_{3}}b(y)\bigr)\right\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x_{3}=\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert b(y)\right\rvert_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}^{2}.(7.1)

In particular, ifb≠0b\neq 0, then the real part of the correspondingx3x_{3}-Fourier mode is non-zero for every phase.

## Proof.

Writeb=a+i​cb=a+icwitha,c∈ℝ3a,c\in\mathbb{R}^{3}. Componentwise, the integrand is
the square ofa​cos⁡(2​π​K​x3+φ)−c​sin⁡(2​π​K​x3+φ).a\cos(2\pi Kx_{3}+\varphi)-c\sin(2\pi Kx_{3}+\varphi).

## Remark 7.4(Relation to the classical resistive scale).

The relationLBε≍εL_{B}^{\varepsilon}\asymp\sqrt{\varepsilon}is the classical
resistive scale identified by Moffatt and Proctor in their topological
analysis of fast-dynamo action[35]. Their argument also
concludes that the magnetic field cannot concentrate in
a vanishing volume when the relative magnetic helicity—a normalised
integral measuring the linkage of magnetic field lines—remains of order
one. We impose no such helicity hypothesis. The common feature is theε\sqrt{\varepsilon}scale, which in the present construction is forced
by exact axial-wavenumber matching.

The first-order operatorCu​B=curl⁡(u×B)C_{u}B=\operatorname{curl}(u\times B)satisfies, for everyη>0\eta>0,‖Cu​B‖2≤C​‖u‖W1,∞​‖B‖H1≤η​‖Δ​B‖2+Cη​‖B‖2,B∈H2.\left\|C_{u}B\right\|_{2}\leq C\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}}\left\|B\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{1}}\leq\eta\left\|\Delta B\right\|_{2}+C_{\eta}\left\|B\right\|_{2},\qquad B\in\mathrm{H}^{2}.

It is therefore infinitesimally bounded relative to the Laplacian, so[21, Theorem III.2.10]shows thatL~ε,u\widetilde{L}_{\varepsilon,u}, with domainH2\mathrm{H}^{2}, generates an analytic
semigroup.

Invariance of the divergence-free (or solenoidal) subspace follows
directly from the curl formulation. For
every sufficiently large real resolvent pointλ\lambda, letf∈Lσ2f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}andB=(λ−L~ε,u)−1​fB=(\lambda-\widetilde{L}_{\varepsilon,u})^{-1}f. Thend=div⁡B∈H1d=\operatorname{div}B\in\mathrm{H}^{1}satisfies(λ−ε​Δ)​d=0(\lambda-\varepsilon\Delta)d=0

in distributions. Testing withddgivesλ​‖d‖22+ε​‖∇d‖22=0,\lambda\left\|d\right\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla d\right\|_{2}^{2}=0,

sod=0d=0. Thus every resolvent on a sufficiently large real half-line

this closed subspace is the part ofL~ε,u\widetilde{L}_{\varepsilon,u}, namelyLε,uL_{\varepsilon,u}with domainH2∩Lσ2\mathrm{H}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}.

By the preceding relative bound and the elliptic estimate‖B‖H2≤C​(‖Δ​B‖2+‖B‖2)\left\|B\right\|_{\mathrm{H}^{2}}\leq C(\left\|\Delta B\right\|_{2}+\left\|B\right\|_{2}), the graph norm ofLε,uL_{\varepsilon,u}is equivalent to theH2\mathrm{H}^{2}-norm.
SinceH2​(𝕋3)↪L2​(𝕋3)\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{3})is compact,Lε,uL_{\varepsilon,u}has compact resolvent. Analyticity and[21, Theorem II.4.29]make the semigroup immediately compact,

## Appendix BEnergy well-posedness

We record well-posedness at the regularity of the final
velocity field, allowing time dependence as well. We first solve the
transport–stretching equation on all vector fields by form methods; a
Galerkin argument then shows that divergence-free data remain
divergence-free and hence recover the physical induction equation.
Related well-posedness and energy estimates for the autonomous whole-space
problem are proved in[15, Lemma 3.1].

## Proposition B.1(Energy well-posedness).

Letε>0\varepsilon>0, letu∈Lloc∞​([0,∞);W1,∞​(𝕋3;ℝ3)),div⁡u​(t,⋅)=0for almost every​t,u\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})),\qquad\operatorname{div}u(t,\cdot)=0\quad\text{for almost every }t,

and letBin∈Lσ2​(𝕋3)B_{\rm in}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{T}^{3}). Then
(1.1) has a unique energy solutionB∈C​([0,∞);Lσ2)∩Lloc2​([0,∞);H1),∂tB∈Lloc2​([0,∞);H−1).B\in\mathrm{C}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\sigma})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathrm{H}^{1}),\qquad\partial_{t}B\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathrm{H}^{-1}).

Here an energy solution means a field in the displayed class withB​(0)=BinB(0)=B_{\rm in}inL2\mathrm{L}^{2}such that, for everyT>0T>0,⟨∂tB,Φ⟩H−1,H1+𝔟t​(B,Φ)=0\left\langle\partial_{t}B,\Phi\right\rangle_{\mathrm{H}^{-1},\mathrm{H}^{1}}+\mathfrak{b}_{t}(B,\Phi)=0

for everyΦ∈H1​(𝕋3;ℂ3)\Phi\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})and almost everyt∈(0,T)t\in(0,T),
where𝔟t​(B,Φ)=ε​⟨∇B,∇Φ⟩+⟨(u​(t)⋅∇)​B,Φ⟩−⟨(B⋅∇)​u​(t),Φ⟩.\mathfrak{b}_{t}(B,\Phi)=\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla B,\nabla\Phi\right\rangle+\left\langle(u(t)\cdot\nabla)B,\Phi\right\rangle-\left\langle(B\cdot\nabla)u(t),\Phi\right\rangle.

## Proof.

We first solve the transport–stretching equation on all vector fields.
The form𝔟t\mathfrak{b}_{t}is measurable inttand bounded onH1×H1\mathrm{H}^{1}\times\mathrm{H}^{1}. Incompressibility and periodicity giveRe⁡⟨(u​(t)⋅∇)​B,B⟩=0,|Re⁡⟨(B⋅∇)​u​(t),B⟩|≤‖∇u​(t)‖∞​‖B‖22.\operatorname{Re}\left\langle(u(t)\cdot\nabla)B,B\right\rangle=0,\qquad\left\lvert\operatorname{Re}\left\langle(B\cdot\nabla)u(t),B\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq\left\|\nabla u(t)\right\|_{\infty}\left\|B\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Consequently, on each finite interval[0,T][0,T], the form satisfies the
Gårding inequalityRe⁡𝔟t​(B,B)≥ε2​‖∇B‖22−Cε,u,T​‖B‖22.\operatorname{Re}\mathfrak{b}_{t}(B,B)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left\|\nabla B\right\|_{2}^{2}-C_{\varepsilon,u,T}\left\|B\right\|_{2}^{2}.

After addingωT​⟨B,Φ⟩\omega_{T}\left\langle B,\Phi\right\rangle, withωT>Cε,u,T\omega_{T}>C_{\varepsilon,u,T}, the form is coercive.

now gives existence and uniqueness inL2​(0,T;H1)\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{1})with time derivative inL2​(0,T;H−1)\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{H}^{-1}). The embedding[33, Chapter III, Section 1, Proposition 1.2]gives
