## Appendix CDetailed comparison with earlier constructions

The labelfast dynamodoes not by itself specify the spatial
setting, the regularity and time dependence of the velocity, whether the
velocity is chosen before the diffusivity, or whether growth holds for all
times, only along a sequence, or only in a limsup. The comparison below
distinguishes resistive from ideal results, deterministic PDEs from
random models and discrete maps, and statements in which one velocity
is fixed beforeε\varepsilonfrom those in which it is not. It also records
whether growth is persistent, appears only in a limsup growth rate, or is
obtained for a selected weak solution on most times.

These distinctions are essential for time-dependent velocities, since the
liminf and limsup growth rates oft−1​log⁡‖B​(t)‖2t^{-1}\log\left\|B(t)\right\|_{2}may differ.
A magnetic eigenmode of an autonomous induction generator, or an eigenvector
of the one-period solution operator (a so-called Floquet mode), instead gives a persistent
exponential rate. General background may be found in[34,13,4].

## C.1.Autonomous spectral mechanisms

At one fixed positive diffusivity, Childress[12]constructed
a smooth autonomous steady-state dynamo in a bounded conducting region, using a rapidly
periodic interior velocity and a boundary correction. The result produces a
nontrivial steady magnetic field, but not the strictly positive growth
required by our terminology. Related spatially periodic
dynamos were studied by Roberts[42].

Ponomarenko’s screw flow[40]is an autonomous helical
velocity in an infinite conducting medium, with a jump at the boundary of a
cylinder. Separation into modeseλ​t+i​m​θ+i​k​z​b​(r),\mathrm{e}^{\lambda t+im\theta+ikz}b(r),

wherezzis the axial coordinate andmmandkkare the angular and
axial wavenumbers,
reduces the induction equation to a radial dispersion relation. Gilbert’s
high-conductivity analysis[25]optimises overm,k≍ε−1/2m,k\asymp\varepsilon^{-1/2}and finds a magnetic layer of widthO​(ε)O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})around the jump, with a positive growth rate of order one.
This is a matched-asymptotic calculation, rather than a rigorous spectral
construction with error estimates. The jump in the rotational and axial velocities,
rather than a smooth shear profile, is central to that asymptotic balance.
Smooth screw profiles were studied in[44], while
Gérard-Varet and Rousset considered regularised helical vortex sheets
that are smooth versions of the Ponomarenko flow[24].

Under their helicity assumptions, Moffatt and Proctor’s topological analysis
likewise identifiesO​(ε)O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})as the resistive magnetic scale associated with fast
dynamo action[35]. In the present theorem this scale is
not only a formal layer width: Corollary7.3bounds theH1\mathrm{H}^{1}-based magnetic length of the global magnetic eigenmode above and below by
constant multiples ofε\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Navarro-Fernández and Villringer[37, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2]give a rigorous spectral construction for a
broad class ofC3\mathrm{C}^{3}helical profiles on cylindrical products, including
the whole plane and radial domains with perfectly conducting boundary
conditions. For every sufficiently smallε\varepsilon, they choose angular and
axial wavenumbers of orderε−1/3\varepsilon^{-1/3}and construct a divergence-free
magnetic eigenmode concentrated in anO​(ε1/3)O(\varepsilon^{1/3})neighbourhood of a
radiusr0r_{0}at which the modal transport phase is stationary,m​Ω′​(r0)+k​U′​(r0)=0.m\Omega^{\prime}(r_{0})+kU^{\prime}(r_{0})=0.

This neighbourhood is the critical layer. The eigenvalue has
positive real part of orderε1/3\varepsilon^{1/3}, with a sharp asymptotic expansion
after a purely imaginary transport phase is removed; that phase changes
oscillation but not growth[37, Section 3.2]. Their result constructs
this particular unstable branch. It does not bound the largest real part of
the full spectrum and therefore does not rule out another branch with
order-one growth for the same profile.

Soward[46]considered an autonomous velocity periodic in two
variables and independent of the third, whose vorticitycurl⁡u\operatorname{curl}uis
singular at hyperbolic stagnation points, meaning zeros of the velocity with
saddle-type linearisation. Boundary-layer asymptotics and a mean-field
approximation for the averaged large-scale magnetic field predict an axial
wavenumber of orderε−1/2\varepsilon^{-1/2}, magnetic sheets of
widthO​(ε)O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}), and order-one growth. For the corresponding bounded-vorticity profile the same calculation gives a rate tending to zero. These
are formal high-conductivity asymptotics.

Compact curved manifolds admit another autonomous mechanism. Arnold,
Zel’dovich, Ruzmaikin and Sokoloff[5]start from a hyperbolic
automorphism of𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}, a torus map with expanding and contracting
directions, and realise it as the return map of a continuous flow on a
compact three-manifold. This construction is called a suspension. An
invariant expanding direction gives an explicit eigenvalue that tends to a
positive limit asε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0. Chicone and Latushkin[10]obtain a rigorous spectral realisation for the geodesic flow on
the unit tangent bundle of a compact surface of constant negative curvature,
with an eigenvalue tending to a positive limit. The ideal kinematic dynamo
operator on compact manifolds is studied in[11]. Vishik[49]relates this mechanism
to exponential stretching of particle trajectories and, under the stated
smoothness hypotheses, rules out fast dynamo action when such stretching is
absent. These examples
use the geometry of a curved compact three-manifold and do not give a smooth
autonomous flow on the flat torus.

Friedlander and Vishik likewise show that the absence of exponential
particle stretching in a smooth steady flow permits only slow dynamo action[22]. Klapper and Young bound the resistive growth
rate by topological entropy, with a finite-smoothness correction[32]; in theC∞\mathrm{C}^{\infty}class, zero entropy rules out a
fast dynamo. These results assume more regularity than is available here:
the present velocity is Lipschitz but notC1\mathrm{C}^{1}. For this same velocity,
Proposition1.4gives
at most linear stretching, zero entropy, and zero ideal exponential growth,
whereas Theorem1.3gives a uniformly positive spectral growth rate for
the resistive operator.
The nonconvergent gradients at the accumulation circle are therefore part
of the mechanism rather than an accidental loss of regularity.

## C.2.Rigorous PDE constructions on Euclidean domains and the flat torus

Coti Zelati, Sorella and Villringer[15, Theorem 1]construct one autonomous
velocityu∈W1,∞​(ℝ3;ℝ3),div⁡u=0,u\in\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad\operatorname{div}u=0,

and constantsc0,γ>0c_{0},\gamma>0such that, for everyε∈(0,1)\varepsilon\in(0,1), anε\varepsilon-dependent finite-energy datum satisfies‖Bε​(t)‖2≥c0​eγ​t​‖Binε‖2(t≥0).\left\|B^{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|_{2}\geq c_{0}\mathrm{e}^{\gamma t}\left\|B^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}\right\|_{2}\qquad(t\geq 0).

Their construction first produces unstable modes of a smooth periodic flow
that are periodic up to a phase factor (quasiperiodic modes). It then places
localised copies at geometrically separated scales. The whole-space localisation uses a second
index for the time horizon: the regions used to localise the solution grow with that
horizon and can be placed arbitrarily far apart. The present proof replaces
that evolution comparison by localisation of the finite-dimensional spectral
subspace defined by a fixed contour, so that one compact cell per diffusivity
scale is enough.

The recent flat-torus results use time dependence in three distinct ways.
Rowan[43, Theorem 1.5]proves that, after any countable set of
diffusivities in a fixed interval has been prescribed, one can choose a
common smooth time-dependent velocity, with all space-time derivatives
uniformly bounded, and one fixed initial datum. For each prescribed
diffusivity, the maximum of the magnitudes of the Fourier coefficients with|k|=1\left\lvert k\right\rvert=1has a uniformly positive limsup growth rate.
The maximising wave
vector may depend on time and diffusivity. The velocity is selected after
the countable set, and the
conclusion does not give a positive liminf growth rate for every
diffusivity.

Sorella and Villringer[45, Theorem 1.1 and Section 6]construct one fully time-dependent
velocity inLt∞​Wx1,∞​(𝕋3)\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{t}\mathrm{W}^{1,\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{T}^{3}), independent ofε\varepsilon, and use
the same constant datum for every diffusivity. For every sufficiently smallε\varepsilon, the magnetic norm has a uniformly positive exponential rate along anε\varepsilon-dependent sequence of
times, whereas its liminf growth rate is zero for every fixedε>0\varepsilon>0. Thus their theorem gives a positive limsup growth rate for
every sufficiently small diffusivity, rather than persistent exponential growth.

Coti Zelati, Sorella and Villringer construct one time-periodic, spatially
Lipschitz velocity, independent ofε\varepsilon[16, Theorem 1]. For every sufficiently smallε>0\varepsilon>0, the
period map—the solution operator over one temporal period, with diffusion
active throughout—has anε\varepsilon-dependent eigenvector (Floquet mode) with
eigenvalue (Floquet multiplier)ρε\rho_{\varepsilon}. There is a constantc∗>0c_{*}>0,
independent ofε\varepsilon, such that|ρε|≥1+c∗\left\lvert\rho_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert\geq 1+c_{*}. The norm identity is exact at integer
multiples of the period, and energy bounds between those times give a
uniform all-time lower bound[16, Proof of Theorem 1, Steps 2–4]. The proof isolates an
expanding spectral component of the ideal stretch–fold–shear transfer
operator, a discrete evolution that successively applies those three
deformations. It uses norms that treat expanding and contracting directions
differently and shows that the expansion persists under continuous diffusion.

## C.3.Other formulations

Several rigorous random models establish growth with different objects and
quantifiers. Kazantsev[31]closes the equation for an ensemble
two-point correlation under a Gaussian velocity that is uncorrelated at
distinct times (white in time). The linear and renovating, or
piecewise resampled, flows of[51,36]yield almost-sure growth or
growth of statistical moments, such as expected powers of the field norm,
in the whole space; in the linear model the velocity is spatially
unbounded. Baxendale and Rozovskii[6]analyse stretching and intermittency—highly
uneven growth across space, time, or realisations—for an ideal stochastic
particle flow. On𝕋3\mathbb{T}^{3}, Coti Zelati and Navarro-Fernández[14]obtain almost-sure ideal growth from randomised versions of the classical
Arnold–Beltrami–Childress (ABC) flows, explicit periodic velocity fields
whose curl is proportional to the field itself. Jiang and Zhang[28]obtain ideal upper-limit growth for random compositions of simpler component
flows. The models in[31,51,36]include magnetic
diffusion, while the works[6,14,28]concern
ideal stochastic flows. Their conclusions are ensemble or almost-sure
statements rather than deterministic spectral statements.

Del Nin, Faraco, Lindberg and Mengual[19, Theorems 1.3 and 1.7]construct, on every smooth bounded
domain, one time-dependent velocityu∈⋂p<∞Lloc1​W1,p.u\in\bigcap_{p<\infty}\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\rm loc}\mathrm{W}^{1,p}.

Their scheme and fast-dynamo conclusions also apply on the periodic domain𝕋3\mathbb{T}^{3}[19, Section 1.2.1]. For every0<ε≤10<\varepsilon\leq 1, the construction selects a weak solutionBεB^{\varepsilon}in
the sense of[19, Definition 1.6]whose prescribed exponential or
superexponential growth holds on a setEεE_{\varepsilon}of times satisfyinglimT→∞|Eε∩[0,T]|T=1.\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{\left\lvert E_{\varepsilon}\cap[0,T]\right\rvert}{T}=1.

This is the density-one property in[19, Definition 1.2 and Section 4.11.1]. At this regularity,
uniqueness is not part of the formulation, and the growth functional
permits optimisation over weak solutions in the stated class.
The well-posedness and time quantifiers of this convex-integration result
differ from those of the persistent magnetic eigenmode
constructed here.

Finally, a separate line of work studies maps and pulsed flows in which
deformation is followed by diffusion. Bayly and Childress[7]introduced stretch–fold–shear maps; Soward[47]studied a periodically pulsed flow; Oseledets[39]related a smoothed torus-map exponent to topological data;
and Pramy, Mestel and Gilbert[41]gave a computer-assisted proof of
an expanding eigenvalue for a one-dimensional stretch–fold–shear operator.
These models are not autonomous generators of the induction equation. Their
time and diffusivity quantifiers therefore do not directly address the
autonomous PDE conjecture.

To the best of our knowledge, no preceding result provides one autonomous
velocity on the flat torus that lies in the Lipschitz well-posedness class
and, for every sufficiently small diffusivity, produces a persistent
magnetic eigenmode with a uniform positive growth rate. The proof combines
two earlier ideas—the instability mechanism of the Ponomarenko screw flow[40,25]and, following[15], the assignment of diffusivity ranges to geometrically
separated scales. The added axial velocities shift the unwanted local spectra
in the imaginary direction, while the fixed Riesz contour allows the matching
local spectral subspace to be transferred to the compact flat torus.

## C.4.Other related constructions

Related localisation constructions also occur outside kinematic dynamo
theory. Sparse complex Schrödinger potentials may be assembled from
weakly interacting local pieces while retaining spectral information[8,17]. Related local-to-global ideas occur in
semiclassical multiple-well analysis and in the gluing of resolvent
estimates[27,18]. On the multiscale
side, fractal homogenisation constructions encode mechanisms at
infinitely many scales, with diffusivity determining the scales relevant
to the argument[2]. These works concern different
operators and conclusions but use related localisation and scale-selection
methods.
